Higher Period Integrals and Derivatives of L L -functions
Shurui Liu, Zeyu Wang

Abstract
. We propose a geometric framework to produce a formula relating higher period integrals to higher central derivatives of L L -functions over function fields, extending the framework [ 4 ] to higher derivatives. For a strongly tempered affine smooth G G -variety X X , we give a geometric construction of the action of L L -observables on the geometric period integral ∫ X 𝕃 σ \int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma} of a Hecke eigensheaf 𝕃 σ \mathbb{L}_{\sigma} on Bun G \Bun_{G} . By taking a suitable version of Frobenius trace of this action, we recover higher central derivatives of the L L -function attached to the dual symplectic representation. As an application, in the Rankin–Selberg case ( GL n × GL n − 1 , GL n − 1 ) (\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},\GL_{n-1}) , we obtain a formula for higher derivatives of the Rankin–Selberg L L -function. This provides a conceptual generalization of the higher Gross–Zagier formula of [ 34 ] to higher-dimensional spherical varieties.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
Background and motivation

1.1.1.
Period integrals and special values of
L
-functions

A central theme in modern number theory and the Langlands program is the relationship between
period integrals
of automorphic forms and special values of
L
-functions. Given a reductive group
G
over a global field
F
, a subgroup
H\subset G
, and a cuspidal automorphic form
\varphi
on
G
, the period integral

(1.1)

\mathcal{P}_{H}(\varphi)=\int_{[H]}\varphi(h)\,dh

is expected to encode special values of
L
-functions attached to the automorphic representation generated by
\varphi
. Prototypical examples include the Waldspurger formula
[
30
]
, relating toric periods on
\PGL_{2}
to
L(\pi,1/2)
, and the Rankin–Selberg integral, relating periods for
\GL_{n-1}\subset\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
to
L(\pi_{n}\times\pi_{n-1},1/2)
.

A conceptual framework for such relations has emerged in recent years. Sakellaridis and Venkatesh
[
28
]
relate periods for spherical varieties to Plancherel decompositions and
L
-functions. The recent work of Ben-Zvi, Sakellaridis, and Venkatesh
[
4
]
further develops this formalism into
relative Langlands duality
: to a hyperspherical Hamiltonian
G
-variety
M
one associates a dual Hamiltonian
\check{G}
-variety
\check{M}
, and the period integral for
M
is expected to be related to an
L
-value read off from
\check{M}
. This framework unifies many known results, including the Waldspurger and Rankin–Selberg cases above, and predicts new ones.

1.1.2.
Special cycles and higher derivatives of
L
-functions

While period integrals capture
values
of
L
-functions, deeper arithmetic information lies in higher
derivatives
of
L
-functions. A prototypical example is the Gross–Zagier formula
[
14
]
, which relates the Néron–Tate height of Heegner points (special cycles) on modular curves to
L^{\prime}(E,1)
for an elliptic curve
E
. Combined with Kolyvagin’s Euler system method, this yields the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture for elliptic curves of analytic rank at most one. More generally, the Beilinson–Bloch–Kato conjectures predict that the order of vanishing of
L(\pi,s)
at the central point equals the rank of a Selmer group, and that the leading Taylor coefficient is governed by arithmetic invariants. These conjectures remain widely open over number fields.

Over number fields, the geometric meaning of higher derivatives of
L
-functions remains mysterious even for elliptic curves, and is far out of reach in higher rank. By contrast, the function field setting provides a fertile testing ground, largely due to the availability of geometric methods, in particular the existence of moduli of Shtukas (with multiple legs), which serve as function field analogues of Shimura varieties.

A first breakthrough came in the work of Yun and Zhang
[
34
]
[
35
]
, who established a
higher Gross–Zagier formula
generalizing the Waldspurger formula: for certain cuspidal automorphic representations
\pi
of
\PGL_{2}
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}(C)
, the self-intersection number of the
\pi
-isotypic component of the Heegner–Drinfeld cycle on the moduli of
\PGL_{2}
-Shtukas with
r
legs is related to the
r
-th central derivative of
L(\pi,s)
. This result has since inspired further developments, including higher theta series and higher Siegel–Weil formulas
[
9
,
11
,
10
]
, as well as arithmetic volume computations
[
12
]
.

However, their approach relies on the compactness of Heegner–Drinfeld cycles, a feature specific to toric periods that rarely holds in higher-dimensional settings. Moreover, their argument is intrinsically tied to the relative trace formula and does not yield a conceptual mechanism applicable in general. In particular, for a general period attached to a
G
-variety
X
, where no relative trace formula is available, it remains unclear why higher derivatives should arise.

1.1.3.
A conceptual and uniform framework in the strongly tempered case

In this article, we develop a framework addressing these questions in broad generality. The term “higher” in the title has a four-fold meaning: higher derivatives of
L
-functions, higher rank groups, higher categorical structures, and a higher vantage point that unifies recent advances. We extend the relative Langlands framework of
[
4
]
in the strongly tempered case to higher derivatives and also the work of Yun–Zhang to higher rank.

The idea can be summarized as follows. We replace the period integral (
1.1
) by its sheaf-theoretic counterpart (
1.2
), which recovers the classical period integral upon taking the trace of Frobenius. These geometric period integrals admit additional natural endomorphisms given by
L
-observables
. Taking the trace of these endomorphisms composed with Frobenius then produces special cycle classes and higher derivatives of
L
-functions.

This perspective connects several developments in the (geometric) Langlands program. The study of geometric period integrals was initiated by Lysenko
[
24
]
; the action of
L
-observables was proposed in
[
4
]
; and the idea of recovering Shtuka-type information via traces is motivated by
[
1
]
. Combining these ingredients yields a uniform and conceptual way to generalize the work of Yun–Zhang within geometric Langlands theory and also extends relative Langlands framework.

1.2.
Main result: a general formulation

In this section, we formulate our main result under a general setup.

1.2.1.
Geometric result

We fix a smooth, projective, geometrically connected curve
C
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
of genus
g\neq 1
, and a split reductive group
G
over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. Let
X
be a
strongly tempered
affine smooth
G
-variety, and consider the natural map

\pi:\Bun_{G}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}.

Example 1.1
.

If
X
is
G
-homogeneous, so that
X=H\backslash G
for some subgroup
H\subset G
, we refer to
X
by the group pair
(G,H)
. Then
\Bun_{G}^{X}=\Bun_{H}
.

Let
\check{G}
be the Langlands dual group over a finite extension
k/\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
. Given a
\check{G}
-local system
\sigma
and a Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
with eigenvalue
\sigma
, define the
geometric period integral

(1.2)

\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}:=H^{*}_{c}(\Bun_{G}^{X},\pi^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})\in\mathrm{Vect}.

This recovers the numerical period (
1.1
) by taking the trace of Frobenius.

Let
K
be the dual
\check{G}
-Hamiltonian space following
[
4
]
(denoted
\check{M}
in
loc. cit.
). The strongly tempered condition means that
K
is a symplectic
\check{G}
-representation. Let
K_{\sigma}
be the local system on
C
associated to
K
and
\sigma
. The symplectic form
\omega_{K}
on
K
induces an orthogonal form
\omega_{M}
on

M=H^{1}(K_{\sigma}):=H^{1}(C,K_{\sigma}),

and hence gives a Clifford algebra
\operatorname{Cl}(M):=\operatorname{Cl}(M,\omega_{M})
.

We now state the main geometric result (see
?THM?
for a precise formulation), asserting that the geometric period integral (
1.2
) carries a Clifford module structure:

Theorem 1.2
.

Assuming
[
4
, Conjecture 8.5.1]
, there exists an action of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
.

Remark 1.3
.

The Clifford algebra action in
?THM?
is predicted in
[
4
, §18]
as an action by
L
-observables
, arising from the conjectural description of the factorizable Plancherel algebra, which is widely open. However, here we only assume
[
4
, Conjecture 8.5.1]
, which only involves
\mathrm{PL}_{X,\hbar}
the (non-factorizable) Plancherel algebra with loop rotation and is known in several important cases, including the Rankin–Selberg case
(G,H)=(\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},\GL_{n-1})

[
7
]
, and the Gross–Prasad case
(G,H)=(\SO_{n}\times\SO_{n-1},\SO_{n-1})
in
[
8
]
. In particular,
?THM?
is unconditional in these cases of central interest in the relative Langlands program.

1.2.2.
Arithmetic result

We now state our formula for the derivatives of
L
-functions. Let
F\in\End(\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})
denote the Frobenius endomorphism, and retain the assumptions of
?THM?
. Let

a:\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to\End(\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})

be the action map. Define elements
z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r}\in(M^{\otimes r})^{*}
by

(1.3)

z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r}(m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r})=\operatorname{tr}\!\big(a(m_{1})\circ\cdots\circ a(m_{r})\circ F,\;\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\big).

We assume that
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
is finite-dimensional so that the trace is well-defined, and that
H^{0}(K_{\sigma})=0
.

We state a rough form of the main arithmetic theorem (see
?THM?
for a precise and more canonical version), which follows from the geometric result and Kolyvagin system formalism in
§3
.

Theorem 1.4
.

Under the above assumptions,

(1.4)

\omega_{r}(z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r},z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r})=\beta_{\sigma,X}(\log q)^{-r}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}\big(q^{(g-1)\dim(K)s}L(K_{\sigma},s+1/2)\big),

where
\beta_{\sigma,X}
is a constant depending only on
X
and
\sigma
, and
\omega_{r}
is the bilinear form on
(M^{\otimes r})^{*}
induced by
\omega_{M}
.

Remark 1.5
.

The elements
z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r}
are expected to correspond to the
\sigma
-isotypic components of special cycle classes in the cohomology of Shtukas. This relation is made precise in the companion work
[
32
]
, under suitable assumptions.

This main arithmetic theorem establishes a new framework to understand the connection between special cycle (classes) and higher derivatives of
L
-functions, and the explication of the constants boils down to explication of the Clifford module structure (i.e. understanding of
L
-observables). As an example to show the power of this framework on higher rank case, we will work out explicitly Rankin–Selberg case.

1.3.
Example: Rankin–Selberg case

We specialize the results of
§1.2
to the Rankin–Selberg case
(G,H)=(\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1},\GL_{n-1})
and explicate the constants. In this setting, one has

K=T^{*}(\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n-1})\in\Rep(\check{G}).

1.3.1.
Geometric result

Fix a geometrically irreducible
\check{G}
-local system
\sigma=(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1})
, where
\sigma_{n}
and
\sigma_{n-1}
have ranks
n
and
n-1
, respectively. By construction in
[
13
]
, we have the Hecke eigensheaf

\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}=\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\boxtimes\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})

with eigenvalue
\sigma_{n}\boxtimes\sigma_{n-1}
.
By
?THM?
, there is a canonical action of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
. In this case, we can explicate this Clifford module structure completely:

Theorem 1.6

(
?THM?
)
.

The Clifford module
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
is the unique irreducible representation of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
.

1.3.2.
Arithmetic result

We now turn to derivatives of
L
-functions. In this case,

L(K_{\sigma},s)=L(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1},s)\,L(\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s).

Theorem 1.7

(
?THM?
)
.

We have

(1.5)

\omega_{r}(z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r},z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r})=\beta_{\sigma}(\log q)^{-r}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}\big(q^{2n(n-1)(g-1)s}L(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1},s+1/2)L(\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s+1/2)\big),

where
\omega_{r}
is the bilinear form on
M^{\otimes r}
induced by
\omega_{M}
, and

\beta_{\sigma}=(-1)^{r/2}q^{-n^{2}(g-1)}\chi^{-n}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}}(\Omega)\chi^{-n+1}_{\det\sigma_{n}}(\Omega)\epsilon(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}).

Here
\chi_{\det\sigma_{n-1}},\chi_{\det\sigma_{n}}:\Pic(\mathbb{F}_{q})\to k^{\times}
are the Hecke characters attached to
\det\sigma_{n-1}
and
\det\sigma_{n}
,
\Omega\in\Pic(\mathbb{F}_{q})
is the canonical line bundle on
C
, and
\epsilon(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1})=\det(\Frob,H^{1}(C,\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1})(1/2))
is the root number.

Remark 1.8
.

?THM?
depends on the choices of Weil structures on
\mathbb{L}^{\mathrm{FGV}}_{\sigma}
, the symplectic form on
K
, and the map
M\to\End(\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}})
, all taken to be canonical. Choosing a Hecke eigensheaf should be viewed as the geometric analogue of choosing a test vector in classical period formulas, with
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
corresponding to a Whittaker-normalized choice.

1.4.
Outline of the proof

We now outline the proof of the main theorems. It proceeds in five steps, corresponding to
§3
,
§4
,
§5
,
§6
, and
§7
, which we briefly summarize below.

1.4.1.
Linear algebra: Kolyvagin systems

The first step (
§3
) is to introduce a general mechanism to deduce arithmetic results (
?THM?
) from geometric results (
?THM?
). The abstract situation can be summarized as follows: Given a Clifford algebra
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
and a finite-dimensional module
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))
equipped with compatible automorphisms
F
, one can construct a sequence of elements
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
called
Kolyvagin system (over function fields)
as in (
1.3
), and get a formula similar to (
1.4
). The Kolyvagin system over function fields is first considered in the work
[
33
]
under a different formulation. Compared to their original formulation, our construction uses the theory of Clifford algebra, which simplifies and conceptualizes the entire picture.

The most important result in this part is
?THM?
, which is a prototype of (
1.4
).

1.4.2.
Commutator relations on cohomological correspondences: local-to-global

Section
§4
provides the first step toward constructing an action of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
: define the action of generators and verify the relations by a local-to-global argument. The generators act via
special cohomological correspondences

c_{V}
on the period sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{X}=\pi_{!}\underline{k}
, which underlie special cycles. We construct
c_{V}
and verify relations by studying their local counterparts
c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\Hom(V,\mathrm{PL}_{X,\hbar})
.

This local-to-global procedure is the focus of
§4
. The main result,
?THM?
, establishes the
commutator relation for special cohomological correspondences
(
?THM?
) under certain technical assumptions (
?THM?
).

1.4.3.
Automorphic Clifford relations

Section
§5
provides the second step toward constructing an action of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
. We single out the minuscule and
Poisson-pure case
(
?THM?
and
?THM?
), which includes most situations where a Clifford action is expected.
?THM?
allows us to construct special cohomological correspondences
c_{V}
via derived fundamental classes. Then the local-to-global procedure of the previous section applies, and the resulting commutator relation, called the
automorphic Clifford relation
(
?THM?
), gives a universal form of the Clifford algebra relations. This section provides a geometric origin of the Clifford action, which is crucial for relating
c_{V}
to special cycle classes in
[
32
]
.

1.4.4.
Higher period integrals and derivatives of
L
-functions

In this section
§6
, we combine all results in previous sections to finally construct an action of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
and apply
§3
to deduce the main arithmetic result
?THM?
, which is the precise formulation of
?THM?
.

1.4.5.
Example: higher Rankin–Selberg convolutions

In this section
§7
, we specialize to the Rankin–Selberg case and explicate all constants. This requires a refined analysis of the Plancherel algebra (see
§7.1
) and a computation of the geometric period integral (see
§7.2
). Combining these ingredients, we complete the proofs of
?THM?
(
?THM?
) and
?THM?
(
?THM?
).

2.
Notations and conventions

In this section, we summarize the notations and conventions used throughout the article.

2.1.
General

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
, define
[n]=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. We use
S_{n}
to denote the symmetric group of the set
[n]
.

2.2.
Categories

By a category, we mean an
(\infty,1)
-category. For a category
\mathscr{C}
, we use
\mathscr{C}^{\omega}
to denote its full subcategory of compact objects. For a stable
\infty
-category (or a triangulated category) with t-structure, we use
\mathscr{C}^{\heartsuit}
to denote its heart. We use
\mathscr{C}^{>-\infty}
to denote the full subcategory of objects bounded on the left.

We use
\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{k}
to denote the
\infty
-category of small idempotent complete
k
-linear categories. We use
\mathrm{LinCat}_{k}
to denote the
\infty
-category of presentable
k
-linear categories with continuous morphisms (i.e., morphisms preserving colimit).

2.3.
Algebraic geometry

In this article, we by default work with schemes/(étale) stacks over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
on the automorphic side, and algebraic stacks over
k=\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
or a sufficiently large finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
on the spectral side. To
X
a stack over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
, we will attach in
§4.1
a category of (ind-constructible) étale sheaves on
X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}}
which we denote by
\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}})
or sometimes we write it as
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
. For
\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
, we write
\Hom(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}):=\Hom_{\mathrm{Shv}(X)}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G})
.

2.4.
Super linear algebra

In this paper, we use
k
to denote a sufficiently large field with
\mathrm{char}(k)=0
, which is usually taken to be
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
(or a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
) for
l\neq p
. In this section, we introduce our conventions on super vector spaces. Although these conventions are standard, the sign involved can be subtle, so we fix the sign rule here.

We use
\mathrm{Vect}
to denote the rigid symmetric monoidal stable
\infty
-category of (complexes of) super vector spaces over
k
, and any linear category would be linear over
\mathrm{Vect}
. We use
\mathrm{sw}:V\otimes W\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}W\otimes V
to denote the commutative constraint of
\mathrm{Vect}
. We use
\mathrm{Vect}^{0}\subset\mathrm{Vect}
to denote the full subcategory of even vector spaces. Regarding
\mathrm{Vect}
as
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
-graded objects in
\mathrm{Vect}^{0}
, we get a natural forgetful functor
\mathrm{oblv}:\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Vect}^{0}
. There is another functor
(\bullet)_{0}:\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Vect}^{0}
defined as taking the even part. Moreover, the category
\mathrm{Vect}
is equipped with an endomorphism
\Pi:\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Vect}
given by reversing the parity, which gives rise to a symmetric monoidal endomorphism
\langle 1\rangle=\Pi[1]:\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Vect}
. We obtain another functor
(\bullet)_{1}=(\bullet)_{0}\circ\Pi:\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Vect}^{0}
that takes the odd part of a super vector space.

For any linear category
\mathscr{C}
and objects
c,d\in\mathscr{C}
, we use
\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}(c,d)\in\mathrm{Vect}
to denote the Hom space enriched over
\mathrm{Vect}
. We write
\Hom^{0}_{\mathscr{C}}(c,d):=H^{0}(\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}(c,d))\in\mathrm{Vect}
. We often drop
\mathscr{C}
if
\mathscr{C}
is clear from the context.

The subcategory of compact objects
\mathrm{Vect}^{\omega}
consists of bounded complexes of finite-dimensional super vector spaces and hence, coincides with dualizable objects in
\mathrm{Vect}
. For each
V\in\mathrm{Vect}^{\omega}
, its (right) dual is denoted by
V^{*}
. There are natural unit and counit maps

\ev:V\otimes V^{*}\to k

\mathrm{coev}:k\to V^{*}\otimes V.

For every morphism
f:V\to W
between
V,W\in\mathrm{Vect}^{\omega}
, the dual homomorphism
f^{*}:W^{*}\to V^{*}
is defined as

f^{*}:W^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}_{V}\otimes\mathrm{id}}V^{*}\otimes V\otimes W^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes f\otimes\mathrm{id}}V^{*}\otimes W\otimes W^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\ev_{W}}V^{*}.

Note that we have

W^{*}\otimes V^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(V\otimes W)^{*}

where the unit and counit maps are given by

\ev_{V\otimes W}:(V\otimes W)\otimes(W^{*}\otimes V^{*})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\ev_{W}\otimes\mathrm{id}}V\otimes V^{*}\xrightarrow{\ev_{V}}k

\mathrm{coev}_{V\otimes W}:k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}k\otimes k\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}_{W}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{V}}W^{*}\otimes W\otimes V^{*}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{sw}\otimes\mathrm{id}}(W^{*}\otimes V^{*})\otimes(V\otimes W).

From now on, we restrict our discussion to
\mathrm{Vect}^{\heartsuit}\subset\mathrm{Vect}
.

For
V\in\mathrm{Vect}^{\heartsuit}
, an
(even) element
in
V
is a linear map
k\to V
, and an
odd element
in
V
is a linear map
\Pi k\to V
. By an element
v\in V
, we mean an element
v\in\mathrm{oblv}(V)
. It is obvious that an even (resp. odd) element of
V
is an element of
V_{0}
(resp.
V_{1}
) and hence can be regarded as an element of
V
. We call an even or odd element of
V
a pure element. For each pure element
v\in V
, we define
|v|
to be its parity, which is zero if
v
is even and one if
v
is odd. Similarly, one defines (even, odd, pure)
coelements
as functionals on
\mathrm{oblv}(V)
.

For any
F\in\End(V)
, we use
\operatorname{tr}(F)\in k
to denote the (super)trace

(2.1)

\operatorname{tr}(F):k\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}}V^{*}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes F}V^{*}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\mathrm{sw}}V\otimes V^{*}\xrightarrow{\ev}k.

In concrete terms, the (super)trace is related to the usual trace of endomorphisms of vector spaces by
\operatorname{tr}(F)=\operatorname{tr}(F|_{V_{0}})-\operatorname{tr}(F|_{V_{1}})
.

For
F\in\Aut(V)
, we define the (super)determinant of
F
(also known as Berezinian in the literature) to be

(2.2)

\det(F):=\det(F|_{V_{0}})\det(F|_{V_{1}})^{-1}\in k.

By a bilinear form between
V,W\in\mathrm{Vect}^{\heartsuit}
, we mean an even coelement of
V\otimes W
. Note that we have a
S_{n}
-action on
V^{\otimes n}
induced by
\mathrm{sw}
. We use

(2.3)

\mathrm{sw}_{i,i+1}\in\End(V^{\otimes n})

to denote the action of the permutation
(i,i+1)\in S_{n}
. The action of
S_{n}
on
V^{\otimes n}
gives a direct sum decomposition

V^{\otimes n}=\bigoplus_{\chi\in\Irr(S_{n})}(V^{\otimes n})_{\chi}.

We define
\Sym^{n}V:=(V^{\otimes n})_{\mathrm{triv}}
and
\bigwedge^{n}V:=(V^{\otimes n})_{\operatorname{sgn}}
. A bilinear form on
V
is called symplectic (resp. orthogonal) if it comes from a coelement of
\bigwedge^{2}V
(resp.
\Sym^{2}V
). For a bilinear form
b:V\otimes W\to k
, we write
b(v,w)=b(v\otimes w)
for
v\in V
and
w\in W
, which are pure elements (get zero if they do not have the same parity).

For
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}\in V
, we write

v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{n}:=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{g\in S_{n}}g(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})\in\Sym^{n}V\subset V^{\otimes n}

and

v_{1}\wedge v_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}:=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{g\in S_{n}}\operatorname{sgn}(g)g(v_{1}\otimes v_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n})\in\Sym^{n}V\subset V^{\otimes n}.

Under this convention, the natural quotient map
V^{\otimes n}\to\Sym^{n}V
is
v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}\mapsto v_{1}\cdots v_{n}
, and the natural quotient map
V^{\otimes n}\to\bigwedge^{n}V
is
v_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{n}\mapsto v_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge v_{n}
.

For each
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, We have
\Sym^{n}V^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\Sym^{n}V)^{*}
where the evaluation map is given by

(2.4)

\ev_{\Sym^{n}V}:\Sym^{n}V\otimes\Sym^{n}V^{*}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes n!}(V^{\otimes n})\otimes(V^{*\otimes n})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(V^{\otimes n})\otimes(V^{\otimes n})^{*}\xrightarrow{\ev_{V^{\otimes n}}}k.

There is a similar construction for
\bigwedge^{n}V^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(\bigwedge^{n}V)^{*}
. One should pay attention to the factor
n!
here.

For any
V\in\mathrm{Vect}^{\heartsuit}
, we have a canonical symplectic pairing
\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
on
V\oplus V^{*}
defined by

(2.5)

\omega_{\mathrm{can}}:(V\oplus V^{*})\otimes(V\oplus V^{*})\to V\otimes V^{*}\oplus V^{*}\otimes V\xrightarrow{\ev-\ev\circ\mathrm{sw}}k.

We introduce the temporary notation
k_{i}:=\Pi^{i}k\in\mathrm{Vect}
.

For each pure element
v^{*}\in V^{*}
, we sometimes identify it with the pure coelement
V\to k_{|v^{*}|}
which is the composition

v^{*}:V\xrightarrow{\otimes v^{*}}V\otimes\Pi^{|v^{*}|}V^{*}\xrightarrow{\ev}k_{|v^{*}|}.

Conversely, given a pure coelement
v^{*}:V\to k_{|v^{*}|}
, we get an element
v^{*}\in V^{*}
via

v^{*}:k_{|v^{*}|}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}}V^{*}\otimes\Pi^{|v^{*}|}V\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes v^{*}}V^{*}.

It is easy to check that these two operations are inverse to each other.

For a bilinear form which is a linear map
b:V\otimes W\to k
, we sometimes identify it with the linear map

(2.6)

b:W\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}\otimes\mathrm{id}}V^{*}\otimes V\otimes W\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes b}V^{*}.

Conversely, suppose we are given a linear map
b:W\to V^{*}
, we obtain a bilinear form via

b:V\otimes W\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes b}V\otimes V^{*}\xrightarrow{\ev}k.

It is easy to check that these two operations are inverse to each other.

From a non-degenerate bilinear form
b:V\otimes W\to k
, we can also identify it with an element
\mathrm{coev}_{b}:k\to W^{*}\otimes V^{*}
by

(2.7)

\mathrm{coev}_{b}:k\xrightarrow{\mathrm{coev}_{W}}W^{*}\otimes W\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes b}W^{*}\otimes V^{*},

which is also the dual map of
b
.

Given two bilinear forms
b_{V}:V^{\otimes 2}\to k
and
b_{W}:W^{\otimes 2}\to k
, we get a bilinear form
b_{V\otimes W}:(V\otimes W)^{\otimes 2}\to k
via

(2.8)

b_{V\otimes W}:(V\otimes W)\otimes(V\otimes W)\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{sw}\otimes\mathrm{id}}V\otimes V\otimes W\otimes W\xrightarrow{b_{V}\otimes b_{W}}k\otimes k\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}k.

We also get bilinear forms

(2.9)

b_{\Sym^{n}V}:(\Sym^{n}V)^{\otimes 2}\to k

and
b_{\bigwedge^{n}V}:(\bigwedge^{n}V)^{\otimes 2}\ \to k
by restricting
n!\cdot b_{V^{\otimes n}}
to the corresponding subspace. One should pay attention to the factor
n!
here.

When
V
is an odd vector space, in concrete terms, we have

(2.10)

b_{V^{\otimes n}}(v_{1}\cdots v_{n},v^{\prime}_{1}\cdots v^{\prime}_{n})=(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}b_{V}(v_{1},v_{1}^{\prime})\cdots b_{V}(v_{n},v_{n}^{\prime})

and

(2.11)

b_{\Sym^{n}V}(v_{1}\cdots v_{n},v^{\prime}_{1}\cdots v^{\prime}_{n})=\sum_{g\in S_{n}}(-1)^{n(n-1)/2}\operatorname{sgn}(g)b_{V}(v_{1},v_{g(1)}^{\prime})\cdots b_{V}(v_{n},v_{g(n)}^{\prime})

for
v_{1},\cdots,v_{n},v_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,v_{n}^{\prime}\in V
.

Given a bilinear form
b:V\otimes W\to k
, there is a canonical symplectic form
\omega_{\mathrm{can}}:(V\oplus W)^{\otimes 2}\to k
defined by

(2.12)

\omega_{\mathrm{can}}(v,w)=b(v,w),\omega_{\mathrm{can}}(w,v)=(-1)^{1+|v||w|}b(w,v),\omega_{\mathrm{can}}(V,V)=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}(W,W)=0,

for
v\in V
and
w\in W
.

2.5.
Lie theory

We use
G
to denote a connected split reductive group over
\mathbb{F}_{q}
. We use
T\subset B
to denote the maximal torus and Borel subgroup. We get a sequence of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{t}\subset\mathfrak{b}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. We use
X_{*}(T),X^{*}(T)
to denote the cocharacter lattice and character lattice, and
X_{*}(T)^{+}
,
X^{*}(T)^{+}
to denote the subset of dominant elements. We use
W_{G}
to denote the Weyl group of
G
. We use
\rho_{G}\in X^{*}(T)
to denote the half sum of all positive roots of
G
.

For each
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
, we use
\lambda^{+}
to denote the unique element in
W_{G}\lambda\cap X_{*}(T)^{+}
and similarly for elements in
X^{*}(T)
. For
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
, we denote
d_{G,\lambda}:=\langle 2\rho_{G},\lambda\rangle
. We use
d\lambda\in\mathfrak{t}
to denote the differential of
\lambda
.

We sometimes use
H\subset G
to denote a connected split reductive subgroup. We use
T_{H}
to denote a maximal torus of
H
such that
T_{H}\subset T
. We regard
X_{*}(T_{H})\subset X_{*}(T)
. For each
\lambda\in X_{*}(T)
, we always use
\lambda^{+}
to denote the
G
-dominant
W_{G}
-conjugate of
\lambda
(rather than the
H
-dominant conjugate).

We use
\check{G}
to denote the Langlands dual of
G
defined over
k
.

When we talk about representations of
G
, we always mean
right

G
-modules. When we talk about representations of
\check{G}
, we always mean
left

\check{G}
-modules.

2.6.
Varieties with group action

We always use
X
to denote a variety with
right

G
-action. We are mostly interested in the case that
T^{*}X
is a
hyperspherical

G
-Hamiltonian space. For its complete definition and properties, we refer to
[
4
, §3]
. We merely summarize some points that we will use in our article.

In this article, we only care about polarized hyperspherical
G
-varieties on the automorphic side (and denote it by
T^{*}X
). When we talk about a hyperspherical
G
-variety
T^{*}X
, we mean an affine smooth
G\times\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-variety
X
such that the
G\times\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-variety
T^{*}X
is hyperspherical in the sense of
[
4
, §3.5.1]
(See below for the
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-action on
T^{*}X
). Here
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
is a copy of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
and the action of
G\times\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
is on the right.

Over an algebraically closed field in characteristic zero, the hypersphericity of
T^{*}X
implies that
X
is spherical
[
4
, Proposition 3.7.4]
such that the generic
B
-stabilizer is connected, and can be written as
X=Y\times^{H}G
for some connected reductive subgroup
H\subset G
and an
H
-representation
Y
. The requirement that
T^{*}X
is
neutral
for
T^{*}X
to be hyperspherical in
loc.cit
implies that the
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-action on
X
is trivial on
H\backslash G
and of weight one on the fibers of
X\to H\backslash G
(i.e scaling action on
Y
). Taking this normalization, the
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-action on
T^{*}X=(T^{*}Y\oplus(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{*})\times^{H}G
is given by weight one action on
T^{*}Y
and weight two action on
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{*}
, trivial on the base
H\backslash G
. In particular, if
X
is
G
-homogeneous, then the
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-action on
X
is trivial.

When we talk about hyperspherical
T^{*}X
, as in
[
4
, §3.8]
, we always assume there exists and fix a
G
-eigenmeasure
\omega
on
X
with eigencharacter
\eta:G\to\mathbb{G}_{m}
. Such an eigenmeasure is automatically an eigenmeasure for
G\times\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
such that

(2.13)

(g,\lambda)^{*}\omega=\eta(g)\lambda^{\gamma_{X}}\omega

for
g\in G
and
\lambda\in\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
. Here
\gamma_{X}\in\mathbb{Z}
is an invariant of the variety
X
(independent of
\omega
) and one can easily computes that

(2.14)

\gamma_{X}=\dim(Y).

We define

(2.15)

\beta_{X}:=\dim(G)+\gamma_{X}-\dim(X)=\dim(H).

2.7.
Hyperspherical duality

When
T^{*}X
is hyperspherical, we use
\check{M}
to denote the dual
\check{G}
-hyperspherical variety for
T^{*}X
constructed in
[
4
, §3]
. We use
\check{G}_{X}
to denote the dual group of
X
. Let
P(X)
be the stabilizer of the open
B
-orbit in
X
.
We say
T^{*}X
is
strongly tempered
if
\check{M}
is a
\check{G}
-linear representation (or equivalently, the dual group
\check{G}_{X}=\check{G}
and
P(X)=B
). We say that
T^{*}X
is
tempered
if
\check{M}
is of the form
\check{M}=(S\oplus(\check{\mathfrak{g}}/\check{\mathfrak{g}}_{X})^{*})\times^{\check{G}_{X}}\check{G}
(or equivalently, the parabolic
P(X)=B
) where
S
is a symplectic linear
\check{G}_{X}
-representation with weight one
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-action.

2.8.
Intersection complex

On a variety
X
, we use
\IC_{X}
to denote its intersection complex, which is normalized in the following way: For a smooth variety
X
, we have
\IC_{X}:=\underline{k}_{X}\langle\dim(X)\rangle
. That is to say: the complex
\IC_{X}
is perverse and has the same parity as
\dim(X)
and is pure of weight zero whenever weight makes sense.

2.9.
Super geometric Langlands

To normalize the (super) Geometric Langlands equivalence

(2.16)

\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\cong\IndCoh_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}}),

we fix

(2.17)

\Omega^{1/2}\in\Pic(C)

and an isomorphism
(\Omega^{1/2})^{\otimes 2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Omega
. We also need to fix
\psi:\mathbb{F}_{q}\to k^{\times}
, which gives us an Artin-Schreier sheaf

(2.18)

\mathrm{AS}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\mathbb{A}^{1})

lying in (naive) cohomological degree 0 and corresponds to
\psi
under the sheaf-function correspondence.

We define the normalized Whittaker sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Whit}}^{\mathrm{norm}}
as follows. Consider diagram

(2.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

where
\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)}
is defined via the Cartesian diagram

(2.20)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{B}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pt}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{T}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\check{\rho}(\Omega)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

We define

(2.21)

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Whit}}^{\mathrm{norm}}:=\pi_{!}q^{*}\mathrm{AS}\langle\dim(\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\omega)})\rangle

where
\dim(\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)})=(g-1)(\dim(N)-\langle 2\rho_{G},2\check{\rho}_{G}\rangle)
.

We adopt the normalization of the (conjectural) Geometric Langlands equivalence in
[
4
, §12.2.1]
such that under the equivalence (
2.16
), the object
\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}*\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Whit}}^{\mathrm{norm}}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
corresponds to the (shifted) dualizing sheaf
\omega_{\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}}}\langle-(g-1)\dim(G)\rangle\in\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}})\subset\IndCoh(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}})
, where
\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}*:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
is the Beilinson’s spectral projector defined in
[
3
, §13.4.1]
, which is the
right
adjoint of the natural inclusion
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
.

2.10.
Geometric class field theory

Consider the map

(2.22)

\AJ_{d}:C^{d}\to\Pic^{d}

defined by

(2.23)

\AJ_{d}(c_{1},\dots,c_{d})=\mathcal{O}(c_{1}+\cdots+c_{d})

and the map

(2.24)

\overleftarrow{h}:C\times\Pic\to\Pic

such that

(2.25)

\overleftarrow{h}(c,\mathcal{L})=\mathcal{L}(c).

For each
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}^{\mathrm{res}}(k)
, we define
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
to be the unique perverse sheaf on
\Pic
such that

(2.26)

\AJ^{*}_{d}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\sigma^{\boxtimes d}\langle g-1\rangle

and

(2.27)

\overleftarrow{h}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\sigma\boxtimes\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}.

2.10.1.
Unramified classical class field theory

For each
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(k)
, we define the associated Hecke character to be

(2.28)

\chi_{\sigma}:=\operatorname{tr}(\Frob,\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\langle-(g-1)\rangle))\in\Hom(\Pic(\mathbb{F}_{q}),k^{\times})

2.11.
Cohomology of classifying stacks

We choose
\mathcal{O}(-1)\in\Pic(B\mathbb{G}_{m})
to be the tautological line bundle on
B\mathbb{G}_{m}
such that for any line bundle
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(X)
defining a map
f_{\mathcal{L}}:X\to B\mathbb{G}_{m}
, we have
f_{\mathcal{L}}^{*}\mathcal{O}(-1)=\mathcal{L}
. This choice gives us an isomorphism
\Gamma(B\mathbb{G}_{m},\underline{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}k[\hbar]
where
\hbar=c_{1}(\mathcal{O}(-1))
.

2.12.
Shearing

For a vector space
V
equipped with a
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}=\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action, we get a weight decomposition
V=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}
. We define
V^{{\mathbin{\mkern-6.0mu\fatslash}}}:=\bigoplus_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}V_{i}\langle i\rangle
.

3.
Linear algebra: Kolyvagin system

In this section, we introduce the linear algebra machinery used to study higher derivatives of
L
-functions of a symplectic local system over the curve
C
.

•

In
§3.1
, we recall the Clifford algebra and fix conventions.

•

In
§3.2
, we introduce the Pin structure, which serves as a preparation for defining the Kolyvagin system.

•

In
§3.3
, we introduce the Kolyvagin system associated to a Pin structure, and prove the key result
?THM?
.

•

In
§3.4
, we introduce and study the Kolyvagin system associated to a module of the Clifford algebra, which will be the Kolyvagin system occurring in
?THM?
,
?THM?
.

Throughout the section, we work over
k=\overline{\mathbb{Q}}_{\ell}
or a finite extension of
\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
such that all the groups and polynomials split. Assume we are in the following setting:

Setting 3.1
.

We fix
(M,\omega)
to be an odd symplectic vector space of dimension
(0|2n)
for
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, where the symplecticity of the pairing means that it is non-degenerate and for any
m_{1},m_{2}\in M
we have

\omega(m_{1},m_{2})=\omega(m_{2},m_{1}).

For our conventions on super vector spaces, see
§2.4
.

3.1.
Conventions on the Clifford algebra

We start by recalling some basic notions on the Clifford algebra.

3.1.1.
Basic structures

Definition 3.2
.

We define the
Clifford algebra
associated to
(M,\omega)
to be

\operatorname{Cl}(M):=\operatorname{Cl}(M,\omega):=M^{\otimes}/I

where
M^{\otimes}
is the free tensor algebra on
M
and the two-sided ideal
I\subset M^{\otimes}
is generated by elements of the form

m_{1}\otimes m_{2}+m_{2}\otimes m_{1}-\omega(m_{1},m_{2})

for all
m_{1},m_{2}\in M
.
1
1

1

It is more common to take
I
to be generated by
m_{1}\otimes m_{2}+m_{2}\otimes m_{1}-2\omega(m_{1},m_{2})
. We discard the factor
2
to get a cleaner formula in
?THM?
. Moreover, the Clifford algebras we are going to consider (as in
?THM?
) are closer to our convention.

Since
\operatorname{Cl}(M)\in\mathrm{Vect}
, we have its parity decomposition
\operatorname{Cl}(M)=\operatorname{Cl}(M)^{\even}\oplus\operatorname{Cl}(M)^{\odd}
.

There is an increasing filtration
\{G_{i}\operatorname{Cl}(M)\}_{i\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
defined by

(3.1)

G_{i}\operatorname{Cl}(M):=\sum_{0\leq j\leq i}\mathrm{im}(M^{\otimes j}\to\operatorname{Cl}(M)).

The graded pieces with respect to the filtration
G_{\bullet}
is
\Gr^{G}_{\bullet}\operatorname{Cl}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Sym^{\bullet}M
. In particular, the top graded piece
\Gr^{G}_{\mathrm{top}}\operatorname{Cl}(M)=\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}M
is an one-dimensional even vector space. From
\omega:M^{\otimes 2}\to k
, we get an induced orthogonal pairing
\omega_{\mathrm{top}}:(\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}M)^{\otimes 2}\to k
as in (
2.9
).

Definition 3.3
.

A
parity
of
(M,\omega)
is a linear map
\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2}:\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}M\to k
such that

(\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2})^{\otimes 2}=\omega_{\mathrm{top}}.

Remark 3.4
.

The name parity is justified by
?THM?
.

A choice of parity
\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top}}
gives a linear map

\operatorname{tr}:\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to k

defined as the composition

(3.2)

\operatorname{tr}:\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to\Gr^{G}_{\mathrm{top}}\operatorname{Cl}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}M\xrightarrow{\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top}}}k.

Note that for each
M
as above, there are precisely two choices of parity
\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2}
differing by a sign.

3.1.2.
Pin groups

We use
\operatorname{Cl}(M)^{\times}
to denote the group of invertible elements in
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
. There are several important subgroups

(3.3)

\hbox to226.57pt{\vbox to53.1pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 113.28471pt\lower-26.5506pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{
