\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

The group
\mathrm{GPin}(M)
is defined as

\mathrm{GPin}(M)=\{x\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)^{\times}|xMx^{-1}\subset M\},

and the group
\mathrm{GSpin}(M)
is defined by

\mathrm{GSpin}(M):=\mathrm{GPin}(M)\cap\operatorname{Cl}(M)^{\even}.

The group
\mathrm{Pin}(M)
can be defined as

\mathrm{Pin}(M):=\{v_{1}v_{2}\cdots v_{k}\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)^{\times}|v_{1},v_{2},\dots,v_{k}\in M\penalty\ \textup{such that}\penalty\ \omega(v_{i},v_{i})=2\}

and the group
\mathrm{Spin}(M)
is defined by

\mathrm{Spin}(M):=\mathrm{Pin}(M)\cap\operatorname{Cl}(M)^{\even}.

One can easily check that
\mathrm{Pin}(M)
and
\mathrm{Spin}(M)
are subgroups by noting that

v^{-1}=\frac{2v}{\omega(v,v)}

for every
v\in M\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)
such that
\omega(v,v)\neq 0
. Moreover, it is well-known that one has

\mathrm{GPin}(M)=\mathrm{Pin}(M)\cdot k^{\times}

and

\mathrm{GSpin}(M)=\mathrm{Spin}(M)\cdot k^{\times}.

We have a natural group homomorphism

(3.4)

\det:\mathrm{GPin}(M)\to\{\pm 1\}

defined by

\det(g)=(-1)^{|g|},

where
|g|
is the parity of
g\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
.
2
2

2

Every element in
\mathrm{GPin}(M)
is pure.
Moreover, there is a canonical map

(3.5)

R:\mathrm{GPin}(M)\to\Or(M)

g\mapsto R_{g}

defined as

(3.6)

R_{g}(m)=\det(g)gmg^{-1}\in M\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)\penalty\ \textup{for $g\in\mathrm{GPin}(M)$ and $m\in M\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)$}.

We also have a map

(3.7)

\lambda:\mathrm{GPin}(M)\to\mathrm{GPin}(M)/\mathrm{Pin}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}k^{\times}

which satisfies
\lambda(cg)=c^{2}
for
c\in k^{\times}\subset\mathrm{GPin}(M)
and
g\in\mathrm{Pin}(M)
.

For each element
v\in M
satisfying
\omega(v,v)\neq 0
, it is easy to see that for every
m\in M
we have

(3.8)

R_{v}(m)=m-\frac{2\omega(m,v)}{\omega(v,v)}v

which coincides with the reflection along
v
.

3.1.3.
Clifford module

It is well-known that there is a unique up-to-parity (super)representation
S
of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
such that the representation map
\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to\End(S)
is an isomorphism. The representation
S
is called the
Clifford module
. We now construct a specific model of the Clifford module.

Construction 3.5
.

Choose a polarization
M=L\oplus L^{*}
such that
\omega=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
as defined in (
2.5
).

•

Define
S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
.

•

The action of
L^{*}\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
S
is given by the linear map
L^{*}\otimes\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}\to\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
defined as multiplication on the left;

•

The action of
L\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
S
is given by the linear map
L\otimes\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}\to\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
defined via

v\otimes v_{1}^{*}v_{2}^{*}\cdots v_{n}^{*}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}(-1)^{i+1}\omega(v,v_{i}^{*})v_{1}^{*}\cdots\widehat{v_{i}^{*}}\cdots v_{n}^{*}.

•

The actions above uniquely extends to an action of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
on
S
.

Note that the polarization
M=L\oplus L^{*}
induces a parity
\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top},L}
given by

\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top},L}:\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}L\otimes\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}L^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}L\otimes(\Sym^{\mathrm{top}}L)^{*}\xrightarrow{\ev}k

where the second isomorphism is defined in (
2.4
).

An easy computation shows the following:

Proposition 3.6
.

The map
\operatorname{tr}:\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to k
induced by
\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top},L}
as in (
3.2
) coincides with the map
\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to\End(S)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{tr}}k
, where the (super)trace
\operatorname{tr}:\End(S)\to k
is defined in (
2.1
).

Remark 3.7
.

From
?THM?
, one sees that a choice of parity is equivalent to a choice of parity of the Clifford module.

We also note the following obvious property of
\operatorname{tr}
:

Proposition 3.8
.

For pure elements
x,y\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
, we have
\operatorname{tr}(xy)=(-1)^{|x||y|}\operatorname{tr}(yx)
.

3.2.
Pin structures

From now on, we assume we are in the following setting:

Setting 3.9
.

Let
(M,\omega)
be as in
?THM?
. Moreover, we fix
F\in\Or(M)
and get a triple
(M,\omega,F)
.

Definition 3.10
.

A
Pin structure
(resp.
GPin structure
) of
(M,\omega,F)
is an element
\widetilde{F}\in\mathrm{Pin}(M)
(resp.
\widetilde{F}\in\mathrm{GPin}(M)
such that

R_{\widetilde{F}}=F,

where
R:\mathrm{GPin}(M)\to\Or(M)
is defined in (
3.5
).

Note that the lifting
\widetilde{F}
in a Pin structure (resp. GPin structure) is unique up to
\pm 1
(resp.
k^{\times}
). We use

(3.9)

\mathrm{Koly}_{F}\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)

to denote the one-dimensional subspace spanned by all GPin structures of
(M,\omega,F)
. We make the following definition

Definition 3.11
.

A
Kolyvagin structure
of
(M,\omega,F)
is an element
\widetilde{F}\in\mathrm{Koly}_{F}
.

Note that the map
\lambda:\mathrm{Koly}_{F}\cap\mathrm{GSpin}(M)\to k
defined in (
3.7
) can be extended to a map

(3.10)

\lambda:\mathrm{Koly}_{F}\to k

by setting
\lambda(0)=0
. The map
\lambda
is quadratic.

The name Kolyvagin will be justified in
§3.3
.

3.2.1.
Semisimple case

We are now going to give explicit descriptions of the Pin structure
\widetilde{F}
in
?THM?
when
F\in\Or(M)
is semisimple. This section is only used in the proof of
?THM?
and
?THM?
. It can be safely skipped during a first reading.

We first consider the case
\det(F)=1
. In this case, there exists a polarization
M=L\oplus L^{*}
such that
\omega=\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
as in (
2.5
) and both
L
and
L^{*}
are
F
-stable. We choose
F
-eigenbasis
(e_{1},e_{2},\cdots,e_{n})
of
L
and get dual basis
(e_{1}^{*},e_{2}^{*},\cdots,e_{n}^{*})
of
L^{*}
satisfying
\omega(e_{i},e_{j}^{*})=\delta_{ij}
. Suppose we have
Fe_{i}=\alpha_{i}e_{i}
for each
i
, we get
Fe_{i}^{*}=\alpha_{i}^{-1}e_{i}^{*}
. Note the following description of
\widetilde{F}
:

Proposition 3.12
.

The Pin structure
\widetilde{F}
is given by

(3.11)

\widetilde{F}=\pm\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}e_{i}^{*}+\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}^{*}e_{i})\in\mathrm{Spin}(M).

Proof.

Note that

\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}e_{i}^{*}+\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}^{*}e_{i}=(e_{i}+e_{i}^{*})\cdot(\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}^{*})

and we have

\omega(e_{i}+e_{i}^{*},e_{i}+e_{i}^{*})=\omega(\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}^{*},\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}^{*})=2,

therefore we know that right hand side of (
3.11
) lies in
\mathrm{Pin}(M)
. To prove the identity, it suffices to check that
R_{e_{i}+e_{i}^{*}}\circ R_{\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}^{*}}
acts by identity on vectors
e_{j},e_{j}^{*}
for
j\neq i
and has eigenvalue
\alpha_{i}
on
e_{i}
and
\alpha_{i}^{-1}
on
e_{i}^{*}
. The first claim is obvious. The second claim follows from the following direct computation:

\begin{split}R_{e_{i}+e_{i}^{*}}\circ R_{\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}^{*}}(e_{i})&=R_{e_{i}+e_{i}^{*}}(e_{i}-\omega(e_{i},\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}^{*})(\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}+\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}^{*}))\\
&=R_{e_{i}+e_{i}^{*}}(-\alpha_{i}e_{i}^{*})\\
&=-\alpha_{i}e_{i}^{*}+\omega(\alpha_{i}e_{i}^{*},e_{i}+e_{i}^{*})(e_{i}+e_{i}^{*})\\
&=\alpha_{i}e_{i}\end{split}.

The computation for
e_{i}^{*}
is similar.

∎

Let us write
F_{L}=F|_{L}
,
F_{L^{*}}=F|_{L^{*}}
and
F_{\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}}
,
F_{\Sym^{\bullet}L}
to be the automorphisms on
\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
,
\Sym^{\bullet}L
. We have the following immediate observations:

Corollary 3.13
.

Let us take

\widetilde{F}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}(\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}e_{i}^{*}+\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}^{*}e_{i})\in\mathrm{Spin}(M),

then the action of
\widetilde{F}
on
S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
is given by

\widetilde{F}\cdot e_{i_{1}}^{*}\cdots e_{i_{k}}^{*}=\epsilon(L,F)^{1/2}\alpha_{i_{1}}^{-1}\cdots\alpha_{i_{k}}^{-1}e_{i_{1}}^{*}\cdots e_{i_{k}}^{*},

where
\epsilon(L,F):=\det(F_{L})^{-1}=\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}
is the root number. In other words, the action of
\widetilde{F}
on
S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
equals
\epsilon(L,F)^{1/2}F_{\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}}
.

Remark 3.14
.

The above choice of
\widetilde{F}
depends on a choice of square root
\epsilon(L,F)^{1/2}
(rather than choices of square roots of individual
\alpha_{i}
). The last claim in
?THM?
is also valid when
F
is not semisimple but admits the
F
-stable polarization
M=L\oplus L^{*}
.

Now we turn to the case
\det(F)=-1
. In this case, we always have
F
-stable decomposition
M=W\oplus W^{*}\oplus k\cdot x\oplus k\cdot y
such that
W\oplus W^{*}
,
k\cdot x
,
k\cdot y

are orthogonal to each other (recall that we are assuming
F
semisimple), in which
\omega|_{W\oplus W^{*}}
is the canonical symplectic pairing, and
\omega(x,x)=\omega(y,y)=2
,
Fx=x
,
Fy=-y
. Let us set
a=(x+y\sqrt{-1})/2
and
a^{*}=(x-y\sqrt{-1})/2
. Take
L^{*}=W^{*}\oplus k\cdot a^{*}
and
L=W\oplus k\cdot a
. This gives a polarization
W=L\oplus L^{*}
, hence we can use
S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
as the model for the Clifford module. We further pick
F
-eigenbasis
e_{1},\cdots,e_{n-1}
of
W
as before such that
Fe_{i}=\alpha_{i}e_{i}
, and obtain dual basis
e_{1}^{*},\cdots,e_{n-1}^{*}
. We have the following proposition parallel to
?THM?
:

Proposition 3.15
.

We have

\widetilde{F}=\pm(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}e_{i}^{*}+\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}^{*}e_{i}))y\in\mathrm{Pin}(M).

The proof is completely analogous to the proof of
?THM?
, so we omit it. We also have the following Corollary parallel to
?THM?
:

Corollary 3.16
.

Let us take

\widetilde{F}=(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}(\alpha_{i}^{1/2}e_{i}e_{i}^{*}+\alpha_{i}^{-1/2}e_{i}^{*}e_{i}))y\in\mathrm{Pin}(M),

then the action of
\widetilde{F}
on
S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
is given by

\widetilde{F}\cdot e_{i_{1}}^{*}\cdots e_{i_{k}}^{*}=(-1)^{k+1/2}\epsilon(W,F)^{1/2}\alpha_{i_{1}}^{-1}\cdots\alpha_{i_{k}}^{-1}e_{i_{1}}^{*}\cdots e_{i_{k}}^{*}a^{*}

and

\widetilde{F}\cdot e_{i_{1}}^{*}\cdots e_{i_{k}}^{*}a^{*}=(-1)^{k-1/2}\epsilon(W,F)^{1/2}\alpha_{i_{1}}^{-1}\cdots\alpha_{i_{k}}^{-1}e_{i_{1}}^{*}\cdots e_{i_{k}}^{*}.

3.3.
Kolyvagin systems

In this section, we introduce a sequence of elements attached to
(M,\omega,F)
after choosing a Kolyvagin structure (see
?THM?
) and parity (see
?THM?
). This sequence of elements was first considered in
[
33
]
. In comparison with the original definition, we provide a new construction of these elements that is considerably simpler and more conceptual. Since this sequence of elements shares some features with the Kolyvagin system over number fields defined in
[
25
]
, it can be regarded as a function field analog of the Kolyvagin system.

3.3.1.
Definition

Definition 3.17
.

Given
(M,\omega,F)
as in
?THM?
, a
Kolyvagin polarization
of
(M,\omega,F)
is a quadruple

(M,\omega,\widetilde{F},\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2})

where

•

The element
\widetilde{F}\in\mathrm{Koly}_{F}
is a Kolyvagin structure for
(M,\omega,F)
defined in
?THM?
;

•

The element
\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2}
is a parity for
(M,\omega)
defined in
?THM?
.

We say a Kolyvagin polarization is GPin (or Pin, Spin, GSpin) if
\widetilde{F}\in\mathrm{GPin}(M)
(or
\widetilde{F}
lies in
\mathrm{Pin}(M)
,
\mathrm{Spin}(M)
,
\mathrm{GSpin}(M)
).

Definition 3.18
.

We define the
Kolyvagin system
attached to a Kolyvagin polarization
(M,\omega,\widetilde{F},\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2})
to be the sequence of elements

\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

where the element
z_{r}
is defined as the coelement

z_{r}:M^{\otimes r}\to k

such that

(3.12)

z_{r}(m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r})=\operatorname{tr}(m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r}\widetilde{F})

for
m_{1},\dots,m_{r}\in M
, and the map
\operatorname{tr}
is defined in (
3.2
).
We say a Kolyvagin system is GPin (or Pin, Spin, GSpin) if the Kolyvagin polarization is GPin (or Pin, Spin, GSpin).

Remark 3.19
.

We remind the reader that by the convention in
§2.4
, for
m_{r}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{1}^{*}\in M^{*\otimes r}
, the corresponding coelement of
m_{r}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{1}^{*}:M^{\otimes r}\to k
is

(3.13)

(m_{r}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{1}^{*})(m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r})=m_{1}^{*}(m_{1})\cdots m_{r}^{*}(m_{r}).

for
m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r}\in M^{\otimes r}
.

Remark 3.20
.

We sometimes extend the Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
to a sequence
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}}
by setting
z_{r}=0
for
r<0
.

3.3.2.
Characterizing properties

In this section, we describe a characterizing property of the Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\}
, which served as the original definition in
[
33
]
. While this perspective will not be used elsewhere in the present article, we include it here for completeness.

Consider the following morphisms:

•

For each
1\leq i\leq r+1
, we have a map
\mathrm{coev}_{i,i+1}:M^{*\otimes r}\to M^{*\otimes r+2}
defined as the composition

\mathrm{coev}_{i,i+1}:M^{*\otimes r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M^{*\otimes i-1}\otimes k\otimes M^{*\otimes r-i+1}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes\mathrm{coev}_{\omega}\otimes\mathrm{id}}M^{*\otimes r+2}

where the map
\mathrm{coev}_{\omega}:k\to M^{*}\otimes M^{*}
is introduced in (
2.7
).

•

For each
1\leq i\leq r-1
, we have a map
\mathrm{sw}_{i,i+1}:M^{*\otimes r}\to M^{*\otimes r}
introduced in (
2.3
).

•

We have a morphism
\mathrm{pF}:M^{*\otimes r}\to M^{*\otimes r}
defined by

\mathrm{pF}(m_{1}^{*}\otimes m_{2}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r}^{*})=(-1)^{r-1}F(m_{r}^{*})\otimes m_{1}^{*}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r-1}^{*}

where
F=(F^{*})^{-1}:M^{*}\to M^{*}
.

Proposition 3.21
.

For the Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
, we have the following facts:

(1)

For each
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
1\leq i\leq r-1
, we have

z_{r}-\mathrm{sw}_{i,i+1}(z_{r})=\mathrm{coev}_{i,i+1}(z_{r-2}).

(2)

For each
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, we have

\mathrm{pF}(z_{r})=z_{r}.

(3)

The properties in (
1
) and (
2
) uniquely characterizes
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
up to scalar. Equivalently speaking, after fixing the parity in
?THM?
, the construction (
3.12
) defines an isomorphism between the one-dimensional vector space
\mathrm{Koly}_{F}
defined in
?THM?
and the vector space of sequences of elements
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
satisfying (
1
) and (
2
).

Proof.

We first prove the following lemma:

Lemma 3.22
.

A sequence
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
satisfies
?THM?

(
1
)
if and only if there exists some element
T\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
such that the sequence
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
satisfies

(3.14)

z_{r}(m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r})=\operatorname{tr}(m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r}T).

for any
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
and
m_{1},\dots,m_{r}\in M
.

Proof of lemma.

Regard the sequence
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
as a single element
z_{\bullet}\in\prod_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}M^{*\otimes r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}(M^{\otimes r})^{*}
. The condition in
?THM?
(
1
)
is equivalent to say that the element
z_{\bullet}
regarded as a functional
M^{\otimes}\to k
vanishes on the linear subspace
I
spanned by elements of the form

m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{k}\otimes(x\otimes y+y\otimes x-\omega(x,y))\otimes m_{k+1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{l}.

This is equivalent to say that
z_{\bullet}
comes from a function
\operatorname{Cl}(M)=M^{\otimes}/I\to k
. Since the bilinear form
\operatorname{Cl}(M)\otimes\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to\operatorname{Cl}(M)\xrightarrow{\operatorname{tr}}k
is non-degenerate, we know that there exists
F\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
satisfying the desired property.

∎

Note that
?THM?
(
1
)
follows directly from the lemma above.

To prove
?THM?
(
2
)
, one notes for every
m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r}\in M^{\otimes r}
, we have

\begin{split}z_{r}(m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r})&=\operatorname{tr}(m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r}\widetilde{F})\\
&=(-\det(F))^{r-1}\operatorname{tr}(m_{r}\widetilde{F}m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r-1})\\
&=(-1)^{r-1}\det(F)^{r-1}\operatorname{tr}(\widetilde{F}\widetilde{F}^{-1}m_{r}\widetilde{F}m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r-1})\\
&=(-1)^{r-1}\operatorname{tr}(F^{-1}(m_{r})m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r-1}\widetilde{F})\\
&=(-1)^{r-1}z_{r}(F^{-1}(m_{r})\otimes m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r-1})\\
&=\mathrm{pF}(z_{r})(m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r}).\end{split}

This concludes the proof of
?THM?
(
2
)
.

To prove
?THM?
(
3
)
, we choose a Pin structure
\widetilde{F}
defined in
?THM?
and suppose we have a system
\{z^{\prime}_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
satisfying conditions in
?THM?
(
1
)
(
2
)
. By
?THM?
, there exists an element
T\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
such that the system
\{z^{\prime}_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
is constructed from
T
via (
3.14
). Then the condition
?THM?
(
2
)
tells us that for every
m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r}\in M^{\otimes r}
, one has

\operatorname{tr}(m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r-1}m_{r}T)=\operatorname{tr}(m_{1}m_{2}\cdots m_{r-1}(T_{0}-T_{1})F^{-1}(m_{r}))

where
T_{0},T_{1}
are the even and odd part of
T
, respectively.
Since this identity holds for any
r
, we know that for any
m\in M
one has
mT=(T_{0}-T_{1})F^{-1}(m)
, which implies that
T\widetilde{F}^{-1}
lies in the (super)center of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
which is well-known to be
k\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)
. This implies that
T
and
\widetilde{F}
differ by a scalar, and we finish the proof of
?THM?
(
3
)
.

∎

3.3.3.
Relation to the Selmer rank

This section is not used elsewhere in the article. It discusses an interesting property of the Kolyvagin system, first observed in
[
33
]
, which also explains its name. We include it here to emphasize this property and to provide an independent proof.

Fix a GPin Kolyvagin polarization
(M,\omega,\widetilde{F},\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top}})
as in
?THM?
and construct the associated Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
as in
?THM?
. We have the following facts:

Proposition 3.23
.

For the GPin Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
, define its
order
to be

(3.15)

\ord(z_{\bullet}):=\min_{z_{r}\neq 0}r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}.

Then there is an equality

\ord(z_{\bullet})=\dim M^{F=1}.

Proof.

Note that
z_{r}\neq 0
is equivalent to
\operatorname{tr}((G_{r}\operatorname{Cl}(M))\widetilde{F})\neq 0
, where the filtration
G_{\bullet}\operatorname{Cl}(M)
is defined in (
3.1
). Therefore, we know that
\ord(z_{\bullet})
is the minimal integer
r
such that
\operatorname{tr}(G_{r}\operatorname{Cl}(M)\widetilde{F})\neq 0
. Suppose
\widetilde{F}\in G_{k}\operatorname{Cl}(M)\backslash G_{k-1}\operatorname{Cl}(M)
, we know that

(3.16)

\ord(z_{\bullet})=2n-k.

On the other hand, the number
k
is also the minimal integer
k
such that
F\in\Or(M)
can be written as a composition of
k
reflections. This description gives

(3.17)

k=2n-\dim M^{F=1}.

Combining (
3.16
)(
3.17
), we get
\ord(z_{\bullet})=\dim M^{F=1}
.
∎

3.3.4.
Relation to
L
-functions

Fix a Kolyvagin polarization
(M,\omega,\widetilde{F},\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top}})
as in
?THM?
and construct the associated Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
as in
?THM?
. We are now going to give an expression of central derivatives of
L
-functions via the Kolyvagin system. To do this, we first recall the definition of
L
-functions and root number in this setting.

Definition 3.24
.

Suppose we are given
(V,F)
where
V
is a finite dimensional (super) vector space and
F\in\GL(V)
,

(1)

We define the
L
-function
of the pair
(V,F)
to be

L(V,F,s):=\det(1-q^{-s}F)^{-1};

(2)

We define the
root number
of
(V,F)
to be

\epsilon(V,F)=\det(F)^{-1}.

where the (super)determinant
\det
is defined in (
2.2
).

Recall that
\omega
is a symplectic pairing on
M
, which can be regarded as an isomorphism
\omega:M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M^{*}
as in (
2.6
), hence gives us a symplectic form on
M^{*}
by transporting
\omega
via the isomorphism. This further induces bilinear forms
\omega_{r}
on
M^{*\otimes r}
.

Remark 3.25
.

We recall our convention for the bilinear form
\omega_{r}
given in (
2.8
). Under the isomorphism
\omega:M\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M^{*}
, the bilinear forms
\omega_{r}
on
M^{*\otimes r}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M^{\otimes r}
is given by

(3.18)

\omega_{r}(m_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r},m_{1}^{\prime}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r}^{\prime})=(-1)^{r(r-1)/2}\omega(m_{1},m_{1}^{\prime})\cdots\omega(m_{r},m_{r}^{\prime}).

Warning 3.26
.

One should pay attention to the difference in ordering elements between formula (
3.18
) and the formula (
3.13
). This difference is
essential
for the following
?THM?
to be true.

The main result in this section is the following:

Theorem 3.27
.

For the Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
associated to a Kolyvagin polarization
(M,\omega,\widetilde{F},\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top}})
introduced in
?THM?
, the following identity holds for any
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
:

(3.19)

\omega_{r}(z_{r},z_{r})=\lambda(\widetilde{F})\epsilon_{n,r}(\log q)^{-r}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}(q^{ns}L(M,F,s))

where
\epsilon_{n,r}=(-1)^{r(r-1)/2+n}
.
Here, the map
\lambda:\mathrm{Koly}_{F}\to k
is defined in (
3.10
).

Remark 3.28
.

It is easy to see that when the Kolyvagin system in
?THM?
is Pin as defined in
?THM?
, both sides of (
3.19
) are independent of the choice of Pin Kolyvagin polarization (i.e., changing
\widetilde{F}
by a sign changes neither side of (
3.19
)).

Remark 3.29
.

When the Kolyvagin system is GPin (i.e., non-zero), the minimal number
r
such that the right-hand-side of (
3.19
) is non-zero is
r=\dim(M^{(F-1)^{\infty}=0})
(i.e., the dimension of the
generalized
eigenspace of
M
with eigenvalue
1
). Comparing with
?THM?
, one sees that if the Kolyvagin system satisfies
\omega_{r}(z_{r},z_{r})\neq 0
whenever
z_{r}\neq 0
(for example, this holds if the pairing
\omega_{r}
has some positivity), then
M^{(F-1)^{\infty}=0}=M^{F=1}
. This is used in
[
33
]
, in which the positivity of the pairing is provided by the Hodge index theorem.

Proof.

For each
r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
, the formula (
3.19
) regarded as an equation for
F\in\Or(M)
defines a Zariski closed subset of
\Or(M)
. Therefore, we only need to prove (
3.19
) for semisimple
F
.

We first show that the identity (
3.19
) is stable under direct sum in
M
. Indeed, suppose
M=M_{1}\oplus M_{2}
is an
F
-stable (orthogonal) direct sum decomposition as symplectic odd vector spaces. We define
\omega_{M_{i}}=\omega|_{M_{i}}
. We can assume that the Kolyvagin system
\{z_{r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
comes from an
F
-equivariant
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-module
S
(see
§3.4
for the definition), which admits an
F
-equivariant decomposition
S=S_{1}\otimes S_{2}
where
S_{i}
is an
F
-equivariant
\operatorname{Cl}(M_{i})
-module. We use
\{z_{M_{i},r}\in M_{i}^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
to denote the associated Kolyvagin system for
S_{i}
. Note that we have a decomposition
M^{\otimes r}=\bigoplus_{0\leq r_{1}\leq r}(M_{1}^{\otimes r_{1}}\otimes M_{2}^{\otimes r-r_{1}})^{\oplus\binom{r}{r_{1}}}
such that
z_{r}|_{M_{1}^{\otimes r_{1}}\otimes M_{2}^{\otimes r-r_{1}}}=\pm z_{M_{1},r_{1}}\otimes z_{M_{2},r_{2}}
for each direct summand
M_{1}^{\otimes r_{1}}\otimes M_{2}^{\otimes r-r_{1}}\subset M^{\otimes r}
. Also note that
(-1)^{r(r-1)/2}\omega_{r}|_{M^{*\otimes r}}=(-1)^{r_{1}(r_{1}-1)/2+(r-r_{1})(r-r_{1}-1)/2}\omega_{M_{1},r_{1}}\otimes\omega_{M_{2},r_{2}}
. By Leibnitz’s rule, a similar formula holds for the right-hand side of (
3.19
). Therefore, the identity (
3.19
) holds for
M
if it holds for
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
.

Now, we can assume
\dim M=2
and apply the computational result in
§3.2.1
. We only do the case
\det(F)=1
and leave the case
\det(F)=-1
to the readers. We can assume
\lambda(\widetilde{F})=1
. In this case, we can do the computation in the setting of
?THM?
. Note that
S=k\cdot 1\oplus k\cdot e_{1}^{*}
in this case. The element
\widetilde{F}
acts on
S
by
\widetilde{F}\cdot 1=\alpha_{1}^{1/2}\cdot 1
and
\widetilde{F}\cdot e_{1}^{*}=\alpha_{1}^{-1/2}e_{1}^{*}
. Since both sides of (
3.19
) are zero if
r
is odd, we can assume
r
is even.

We first compute the left-hand side of (
3.19
). When
r=0
, we have
z_{0}=\alpha_{1}^{1/2}-\alpha_{1}^{-1/2}
, hence,
\omega_{0}(z_{0},z_{0})=(\alpha_{1}^{1/2}-\alpha_{1}^{-1/2})^{2}=-(2-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{-1})
. When
r>0
, since
(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}^{*})^{\otimes r/2}\cdot 1=1
and
(e_{1}\otimes e_{1}^{*})^{\otimes r/2}\cdot e_{1}^{*}=0
, we have
z_{r}((e_{1}\otimes e_{1}^{*})^{\otimes r/2})=\alpha_{1}^{1/2}
. Similarly, we have
z_{r}((e_{1}^{*}\otimes e_{1})^{\otimes r/2})=-\alpha_{1}^{-1/2}
. Since
z_{r}
takes zero value on other basis vectors of
M^{\otimes r}
, we know
\omega_{r}(z_{r},z_{r})=(-1)^{r(r-1)/2+1}2
.

Now we compute the right-hand side of (
3.19
). Note that
q^{-s}L(M,F,s)=q^{s}+q^{-s}-\alpha_{1}-\alpha^{-1}_{1}
. Therefore, we have

\epsilon_{n,r}(\log q)^{-r}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}(q^{ns}L(M,F,s))=\left\{\begin{aligned} -(2-\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{-1})&,r=0\\
(-1)^{r(r-1)/2+1}(1+(-1)^{r})&,r>0\end{aligned}\right.

Comparing the computational results above, the identity (
3.19
) is proved.
∎

Remark 3.30
.

One can give an alternative proof of
?THM?
by interpreting the pairing value
\omega_{r}(z_{r},z_{r})
as a single trace: Consider the canonical element
\mathrm{coev}\in M\otimes M
determined by the form
\omega
. It is easy to write
\omega_{r}(z_{r},z_{r})=\operatorname{tr}_{2}((\mathrm{coev})^{r}\widetilde{F}_{2})
where
\operatorname{tr}_{2}:\operatorname{Cl}(M\oplus M)\to k
is the map (
3.2
) for
M\oplus M
and
\widetilde{F}_{2}\in\operatorname{Cl}(M\oplus M)
is some lifting of
(F,F)\in\mathrm{O}(M\oplus M)
. The rest of the proof is again an explicit computation. This alternative proof highlights a similarity between the pairing
\omega_{r}(z_{r},z_{r})
and the arithmetic volume of Shtukas, defined as a trace in
[
12
]
. This perspective also extends beyond the strongly tempered cases considered here, and we plan to return to it in future work.

3.4.
Kolyvagin systems from modules

In
§3.3
, we introduced the notion of the Kolyvagin system associated to a Kolyvagin polarization
(M,\omega,\widetilde{F},\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2})
defined in
?THM?
. In this section, for each
(M,\omega,F)
in
?THM?
, we introduce the Kolyvagin system associated to a finite-dimensional
F
-equivariant module
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F}
.

For
F\in\Or(M)
, we denote its induced automorphism on
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
by
F_{\operatorname{Cl}(M)}
.

Definition 3.31
.

Fix a triple
(M,\omega,F)
in
?THM?
. We define the category of
F
-equivariant modules of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
to be

\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{F}:=\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{F_{\operatorname{Cl}(M)}^{*}}

in which the perfect complexes form the full subcategory

\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F}\subset\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{F}.

In concrete terms, given an object in
\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{F}
is equivalent to give an object
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))
together with an automorphism of perfect complexes
F_{T}:T\to T
such that

(3.20)

F(x)\cdot F_{T}(t)=F_{T}(x\cdot t)

for any
x\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
and
t\in T
.

Definition 3.32
.

For each
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F}
, we get a map of algebras
a:\operatorname{Cl}(M)\to\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(T)
. We define the Kolyvagin system associated to
T
to be sequence of elements

\{z_{T,r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

where the element
z_{r}
is defined as the coelement

z_{T,r}:M^{\otimes r}\to k

such that

(3.21)

z_{T,r}(m_{1}\otimes m_{2}\otimes\cdots\otimes m_{r})=\operatorname{tr}(a(m_{1})\circ\cdots\circ a(m_{r})\circ F_{T}).

Now we fix a Pin Kolyvagin polarization as defined in
?THM?
. The parity
\omega^{1/2}_{\mathrm{top}}
gives a choice of parity on the Clifford module
S
. The Pin structure
\widetilde{F}
defines an
F
-equivariant structure on
S
. From now on, we assume
\det(F)=1
. In this case, we are going to construct a Kolyvagin structure
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}(T)\in\mathrm{Koly}_{F}
(see
?THM?
for definition) such that the system in
?THM?
is the Kolyvagin system attached to the Kolyvagin polarization
(M,\omega,\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}(T),\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2})
.

Since
\operatorname{Cl}(M)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\End(S)
, we have Morita equivalence

(3.22)

\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Vect}

where the equivalence is given by

T\leftrightarrow\Hom_{\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))}(S,T)

V\otimes S\leftrightarrow V

In particular, for each
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F}
, we get
V_{T}:=\Hom_{\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))}(S,T)\in\mathrm{Vect}
such that
T=S\otimes V_{T}
.

Note the following lemma:

Lemma 3.33
.

When
\det(F)=1
, the
F
-equivariant
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-module automorphism
F_{T}\in\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(T)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\operatorname{Cl}(M)\otimes\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(V_{T})
lies in the subspace

\mathrm{Koly}_{F}\otimes\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(V_{T})\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)\otimes\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(V_{T}).

Proof of
?THM?
.

By (
3.20
), an element
f\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)\otimes\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(V_{T})
is
F
-equivariant if and only if

(3.23)

F(x)f=fx

for all
x\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
. Here we are using the natural
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-bimodule structure on
\operatorname{Cl}(M)\otimes\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(V_{T})
(trivial on the second factor). By choosing a Pin structure
\widetilde{F}
as in
?THM?
, we can rewrite (
3.23
) as

\widetilde{F}x\widetilde{F}^{-1}f=fx,

which further rewrites to

x(\widetilde{F}^{-1}f)=(\widetilde{F}^{-1}f)x

that holds for every
x\in\operatorname{Cl}(M)
. Then we are done since the center of
\mathrm{oblv}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))
is
k\subset\operatorname{Cl}(M)
.
∎

Remark 3.34
.

Note that
?THM?
gives a bijection between isomorphism classes of
F
-equivariant
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-modules and matrices with elements in
\mathrm{Koly}_{F}
when
\det(F)=1
.

Then we have the following immediate corollary:

Corollary 3.35
.

Assume
\det(F)=1
. For each
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F}
, consider the map
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}:=\mathrm{id}\otimes\operatorname{tr}:\mathrm{Koly}_{F}\otimes\End_{\mathrm{Vect}}(V_{T})\to\mathrm{Koly}_{F}
which is taking trace on the second factor. Then we get

\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}(T):=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}(F_{T})\in\mathrm{Koly}_{F}.

The system

\{z_{T,r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

in
?THM?
is the Kolyvagin system attached to the Kolyvagin polarization
(M,\omega,\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}(F_{T}),\omega_{\mathrm{top}}^{1/2})
via
?THM?
.

Remark 3.36
.

When
T
is irreducible (i.e., isomorphic to the Clifford module
S
up to parity), the element
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}(T)
is non-zero since
F_{T}
is non-zero. This implies that the Kolyvagin system
\{z_{T,r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
is non-zero (i.e., is a GPin Kolyvagin system).

Given
?THM?
, one can apply
?THM?
to relate the system
\{z_{T,r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}
with
L
-functions. For each
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F}
, we can now define

(3.24)

\lambda(T):=\lambda(\operatorname{tr}_{\mathrm{Koly}}(T))

where
\lambda:\mathrm{Koly}_{F}\to k
is defined in (
3.10
). The number
\lambda(T)
is the most important invariant of an
F
-equivariant module of
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
for us.

3.4.1.
Computing
\lambda(T)

The result in this section will not be used until
?THM?
.

We do not have a general way to compute the invariant
\lambda(T)
unless we get very explicit knowledge of the
F
-equivariant module
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F}
. Even if
T
is isomorphic to the Clifford module as a
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-module, we still need to know about its
F
-equivariant structure. In this section, we introduce a particular case where we can compute
\lambda(T)
easily. For this, we assume we are in the following setting:

Setting 3.37
.

Given
(M,\omega,F)
as in
?THM?
. Moreover, we fix an
F
-stable polarization
M=L\oplus L^{*}
.

In this case, we have the following

Proposition 3.38
.

Suppose we are in
?THM?
, and
S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
is the Clifford module constructed in
?THM?
, equipped with its natural
F
-equivariant structure
F_{\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}}
. Then we have

\lambda(\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*})=\epsilon(L,F)^{-1}

where
\epsilon(L,F)
is the root number defined in
?THM?

(
2
)
.

Proof.

By a Zariski dense argument, we are reduced to the case that
F
is semisimple. Then we are done by
?THM?
.
∎

Therefore, we can compute
\lambda(T)
by relating
T
to the module
S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}
. For this purpose, we introduce the following lemma:

Lemma 3.39
.

Suppose we are in
?THM?
. Given an
F
-equivariant module
T\in\mathrm{Mod}(\operatorname{Cl}(M))^{\omega,F,\heartsuit}
of dimension
(2^{n-1}|2^{n-1})
. Suppose there exists a
F_{T}
-eigen highest weight vector
t\in T
in the sense that:

(1)

We have
F_{T}(t)=\alpha t
for some
\alpha\in k^{\times}
;

(2)

We have
L\cdot t=0

Then the natural action map

a:S=\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}\to\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*}\cdot t\subset T

defines an isomorphism of
F
-equivariant modules after we replace the
F
-equivariant structure on
T
by
F_{T}/\alpha
. In particular, we have

\lambda(T)=\alpha^{2}\lambda(\Sym^{\bullet}L^{*})=\alpha^{2}\epsilon(L,F)^{-1}.

Proof of
?THM?
.

It is easy to see that the map
a:S\to T
is compatible with
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-module structures by (
2
) and is
F
-equivariant after replacing
F_{T}
by
F_{T}/\alpha
given (
1
), therefore must be an isomorphism of
F
-equivariant
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-modules since both sides have the same dimension. The final statement is then a consequence of
?THM?
.
∎

4.
Commutator relations on cohomological correspondences

In this section, we introduce the local-to-global construction of special cohomological correspondences. In particular, relations between local special cohomological correspondences (commutator relations) would give relations on global special cohomological correspondences. These relations are what we call the commutator relations on global special cohomological correspondences. The main result in this section is
?THM?
(proved under technical assumptions in
?THM?
).

•

In
§4.1
, we clarify the étale sheaf theory we are using and establish some properties of it which are well-known to the experts but we cannot find in the literature.

•

In
§4.2
, we review the notion of cohomological correspondences used in
[
10
]
and make some enhancements in our setting.

•

In
§4.3
, we introduce the moduli spaces and categories involved in the local-to-global procedure.

•

In
§4.4
, we make the local-to-global construction of special cohomological correspondences.

•

In
§4.5
, we state the commutator relation on global special cohomological correspondences, which is the main result of this section.

•

Finally, in
§4.6
, we prove the main theorem
?THM?
.

4.1.
Sheaf theory

We now come to set up the étale sheaf theory that we will use throughout the article. Experienced readers can safely skip this part and come back when necessary.

The local-to-global construction of cohomological correspondences, which we are going to introduce in
§4.4
, is essentially part of the automorphic factorizable
\Theta
-series construction discussed in
[
4
, §16]
. It has been observed in
loc.cit
that this construction encounters some difficulties in sheaf theory, and has not been made rigorous at this stage as far as we know. Establishing the full version of this construction is stated as an open problem in
[
4
, Problem 16.3.2]
. We do not try to address this problem in its full generality. Instead, we only establish part of it, which is enough for our purpose (i.e., to see the Clifford relation in the RTF-algebra).

For this purpose, we need a
*
-sheaf theory
3
3

3

By
*
-sheaf theory, we mean a theory in which the upper-
*
functor is always defined.
(to study the
!
-period sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{X}
) on non-Artin and non-placid stacks like
LX/L^{+}G
(and its moving point variants like
(LX/L^{+}G)_{C}
). This is the sheaf theory
\mathrm{Shv}
we are going to produce in this section. Note that although the entire category
\mathrm{Shv}
can be very bad on general stacks, the six-functor functoriality should be mostly well-behaved, which is enough for our purpose.

4.1.1.
Six-functor formalism of ind-constructible sheaves

We briefly review the six-functor formalism of étale sheaf theory with
k=\mathbb{Q}_{\ell}
-coefficients, which will be used later.

We use
\mathrm{Sch}
to denote the category of (classical) schemes over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, and
\mathrm{Stack}
to be the category of (étale) stacks over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
.

Let
X
be a scheme of finite type over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, the (infinity-categorical enhancement of) derived category of constructible complexes
\mathrm{Shv}_{c}(X,k)
has been constructed in
[
19
]
(for torsion coefficients) and
[
18
]
for adic coefficients via right Kan extension along embedding torsion rings into adic rings,
4
4

4

Actually in
loc. cit
, it is even constructed for Artin stacks locally of finite type.
which extends to a lax symmetric monoidal functor

(4.1)

\mathrm{Shv}_{c}:\mathrm{Corr}(\mathrm{Sch}_{\textup{ft}})_{E=\textup{fp}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Cat}^{\mathrm{Idem}}_{k}.

where
ft
is short for finite type schemes and
fp
is short for of finite presentation morphisms. We use
qcqs
short for quasi-compact and quasi-separated.

Using left Kan extension along
\mathrm{Sch}_{\textup{ft}}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset\mathrm{Sch}_{\mathrm{qcqs}}^{\mathrm{op}}
, and applying ind-completion
\mathrm{Shv}(-):=\mathrm{Ind}(\mathrm{Shv}_{c}(-,))
, we obtain a six functor formalism (in the sense of
[
15
, Definition 3.2.1]
5
5

5

Our
\mathrm{Corr(\mathcal{C})_{E}}
is the same as
\mathrm{Corr}(\mathcal{C},E)^{\otimes}
in
[
15
]
.
)

(4.2)

\mathrm{Shv}:\mathrm{Corr}(\mathrm{Sch}_{\textup{qcqs}})_{E=\textup{fp}}\rightarrow\mathrm{LinCat}_{k},

Now we extend the six-functor formalism to étale stacks. Let
P
be a property of morphisms (of quasi-compact quasi-separated schemes) that is stable under base change. In this case, we say a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
has property
P
if
f
is representable and any base change of
f
along a map from a qcqs scheme
S\rightarrow Y
has property
P
.

Then we use the right Kan extension along the full embedding
\mathrm{Sch}^{\mathrm{op}}\subset\mathrm{Stack}^{\mathrm{op}}
. By
[
15
, Theorem 3.4.11]
, we obtain a six-functor formalism

(4.3)

\mathrm{Shv}:\mathrm{Corr}(\mathrm{Stack})_{E=\textup{fp}}\rightarrow\mathrm{LinCat}_{k},

whose restriction to qcqs schemes is the same as the one we constructed before.

More concretely, given a qcqs scheme
S=\varprojlim_{i}S_{i}
where
S_{i}
are schemes of finite-type, we have
\mathrm{Shv}(S)\cong\Ind\varinjlim_{i}\mathrm{Shv}_{c}(S_{i})
, where the transition maps are
*
-pull. For an étale stack
X=\varinjlim_{i}X_{i}
where
X_{i}\in\mathrm{Sch}_{\mathrm{qcqs}}
, we have
\mathrm{Shv}(X)\cong\varprojlim\mathrm{Shv}(X_{i})
, where the transition maps are
*
-pull.

For
f:X\rightarrow Y
a morphism of stacks, we always have
f^{*}
, which admits a (not necessarily continuous) right adjoint
f_{*}
. If
f
is fp, we have
f_{!}
, which admits a (not necessarily continuous) right adjoint
f^{!}
. We also have a right adjoint
\underline{\mathrm{Hom}}
to the tensor product
\otimes
on
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
. In this case, these functors satisfy various compatibilities such as projection formulas, proper base change, etc. We refer to
[
15
, Proposition 3.2.2]
for a complete list of such compatibilities.

Moreover, for a morphism between stacks
f:X\to Y
:

(1)

If
f
is proper, then
f
is
D
-proper in the sense of
[
15
, Definition 4.6.1(a)]
. In particular, we have a canonical isomorphism
f_{!}\cong f_{*}
;

(2)

If
f
is étale, then
f
is
D
-étale in the sense of
[
15
, Definition 4.6.1(b)]
. In particular, we have a canonical isomorphism
f^{*}\cong f^{!}
.

These claims follow directly from
[
15
, Lemma 4.5.7]
and the right cancellative property of proper and étale maps.

Remark 4.1
.

This is a
*
-sheaf theory in the sense of
[
15
, Remark 1.3.10]
.

Suppose
X
is an Artin stack locally of finite type, the following proposition shows that
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
is the same as the sheaf theory defined in
[
3
, §F.1.1]
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let
X=\varinjlim_{\alpha}X_{\alpha}
be a filtered colimit of stacks with transition maps
j_{\alpha\beta}:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X_{\beta}
quasi-compact open immersions. Then

(4.4)

\varinjlim_{\alpha}\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\alpha})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{Shv}(X)

is an equivalence, where the transition maps are given by
!
-push.

Proof.

By
[
23
, Proposition 6.2.1.9]
,
\mathrm{Shv}(X)\cong\varprojlim_{\alpha}\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\alpha})
, with the transition maps given by
*
-pull. Since each
j_{\alpha}:X_{\alpha}\rightarrow X_{\beta}
is a quasi-compact open immersion,
j_{\alpha\beta}^{*}
has continuous left adjoint
j_{\alpha\beta,!}
. Therefore, by
[
21
, Corollary 5.5.3.4, Theorem 5.5.3.18]
, we have

\varprojlim_{\alpha}\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\alpha})\cong\varinjlim_{\alpha}\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\alpha}),

where the transition maps on the right-hand side are given by
j_{\alpha\beta,!}
.
∎

4.1.2.
Pro-smooth base change

Definition 4.3
.

We say a morphism
f:X\rightarrow Y
of quasi-compact quasi-separated schemes is
elementary pro-smooth
(pro-smooth for short), if there exists a presentation
X\cong\varprojlim X_{i}
as a cofiltered limit of qcqs schemes with smooth affine surjective transition maps, such that every map
X_{i}\rightarrow Y
is smooth.

The class of elementary pro-smooth is stable under base change, and hence this notion extends to stacks, i.e., a morphism of stacks is called elementary pro-smooth if it is representable and its base change to any scheme is elementary pro-smooth.

Example 4.4
.

If
X
is an affine smooth scheme over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
, then the jet scheme
L^{+}X
(see
§4.3.1
) is elementary pro-smooth.

Example 4.5
.

Let
G
be an affine smooth group scheme, and let
Z
be a stack equipped with an
L^{+}G
-action. Then the natural map of stacks
Z\rightarrow Z/L^{+}G
is elementary pro-smooth.

Example 4.6
.

Let
D=\Spec\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}[[t]]
be the formal disc and
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
the automorphism group of
D
preserving the center of the disc. Then
\mathrm{Aut}(D)
is pro-smooth over
\overline{\mathbb{F}}_{q}
. In particular, the map of local coordinate
\mathrm{Coor}:C\rightarrow B\mathrm{Aut}(D)
(see
§4.3.1
) is pro-smooth.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
f:X\rightarrow Y
be a representable surjection of stacks. Then
f^{*}
is conservative.

Proof.

When
X
and
Y
are qcqs schemes, the argument in
[
36
]
[Lemma 10.27] works.
In the general case,
\mathrm{Shv}(Y)\cong\varprojlim_{S\rightarrow Y}\mathrm{Shv}(S)
and
\mathrm{Shv}(X)\cong\varprojlim_{S\rightarrow Y}\mathrm{Shv}(S\times_{Y}X)
, where
S
is a qcqs scheme and all transition maps in the limits above are given by
*
-pullbacks. Then it follows from the scheme case by passing to limits.
∎

Lemma 4.8
.

Given a Cartesian square of qcqs schemes

(4.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

with
g
elementary pro-smooth.

(1)

If
f
is of finite presentation, then
g^{\prime*}f^{!}\cong f^{\prime!}g^{*}
.

(2)

We always have that
g^{*}f_{*}\cong f^{\prime}_{*}g^{\prime*}
.

Proof.

This has been proved in
[
36
]
[Proposition 10.32, Corollary  10.46] and we reproduce the proof here. Since all functors involved are continuous, we only need to verify
(g^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}\mathcal{F}\cong(f^{\prime})^{!}g^{*}\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{c}(Z)
. Since
g:Y\to Z
is elementary pro-smooth, we can write
Z=\varprojlim_{i}Z_{i}
and
Y=\varprojlim_{i}Y_{i}
such that all
Y_{i},Z_{i}
are schemes of finite type and the map
g
is induced from a compatible system of smooth morphisms
g_{i}:X_{i}\to Z_{i}
. Since
f
is fp, we can assume that there exists
f_{i}:X_{i}\to Z_{i}
such that
X=X_{i}\times_{Z_{i}}Z
for each
i
. We obtain the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}\times_{Z_{i}}Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{W,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{X,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{Y,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{Z,i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{i}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{i}}

where all squares except the left and right faces are Cartesian.

Assume
\mathcal{F}
comes from
\mathcal{F}_{0}\in\mathrm{Shv}(Z_{0})
, we use
\mathcal{F}_{i}
to denote the
*
-pull of
\mathcal{F}_{0}
to
Z_{i}
. Note we have

f^{!}\mathcal{F}\cong\varinjlim_{i}r_{X,i}^{*}f_{i}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{i}

and

f^{\prime!}g^{*}\mathcal{F}\cong\varinjlim_{i}r_{W,i}^{*}f_{i}^{\prime!}g_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i},

which gives

g^{\prime*}f^{!}\mathcal{F}\cong\varinjlim_{i}g^{\prime*}r_{X,i}^{*}f_{i}^{!}\mathcal{F}_{i}\cong\varinjlim_{i}r_{W,i}^{*}f_{i}^{\prime!}g_{i}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{i}\cong f^{\prime!}g^{*}\mathcal{F}.

Part (
2
) is proved
mutatis mutandis
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Given a Cartesian square of stacks

(4.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{Y}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where
g
and
g^{\prime}
are surjective pro-smooth,
X
and
Y
are qcqs schemes.

(1)

If
f
is representable of finite presentation, then
g^{\prime*}f^{!}\cong f^{\prime!}g^{*}
;

(2)

We always have that
g^{*}f_{*}\cong f^{\prime}_{*}g^{\prime*}
.

Proof.

Let
X_{\bullet}
denote the Cech nerve of
g^{\prime}
, where
X_{n}
are qcqs schemes and all face maps are surjective pro-smooth. Let
X_{\bullet}^{+}
be the underlying semi-simplicial diagram (i.e., discarding all non-injective maps in
\Delta^{\textup{op}}
). We always have
\varprojlim\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\bullet})\cong\mathrm{Shv}(X)
, with transition maps given by
*
-pullbacks. By
[

, Lemma 6.5.3.7]
, we also have that
\varprojlim\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\bullet})\cong\varprojlim\mathrm{Shv}(X_{\bullet}^{+})
. Similarly for
Y_{\bullet}^{+}
. Now by
?THM?
, all the vertical maps in the following diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(Y)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(Y_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(Y_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(X_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Shv}(X_{3})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime!}}

intertwine with face maps, and hence (

) holds by
[

. Similarly, (

) is proved.
∎

For applications, we further generalize pro-smooth base change to the “locally pro-smooth” case.

Proposition 4.10

(Locally pro-smooth base change)
.

Suppose that we have a Cartesian square of stacks

(4.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{W}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{X}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{Y}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathscr{Z}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

Assume that
\mathscr{Y}
admits a pro-smooth surjection
\gamma:Y\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
, such that
Y
is a quasi-compact quasi-separated scheme, and the composition
\delta:Y\xrightarrow{\gamma}\mathscr{Y}\xrightarrow{g}\mathscr{Z}
is also a pro-smooth surjection.

(1)

If
f
is representable of finite presentation, then we have
(g^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}\cong(f^{\prime})^{!}g^{*}
.

(2)

We always have
g^{*}f_{*}\cong f^{\prime}_{*}(g^{\prime})^{*}
.

Proof.

Part (
1
). Let
\gamma^{\prime}:W:=Y\times_{\mathscr{Y}}\mathscr{W}\rightarrow\mathscr{W}
be the base change of
\gamma
along
\mathscr{W}\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}
. Then
\gamma^{\prime}
is a pro-smooth surjection. Since
\gamma^{\prime}
is surjective,
(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}
is conservative by
?THM?
, and we can check
(g^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}\cong(f^{\prime})^{!}g^{*}
by pullback along
\gamma^{\prime}
, i.e. we only need to show that

(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}(g^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}\cong(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}(f^{\prime})^{!}g^{*}.

The three squares in

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces W}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{Z}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

are all Cartesian.

On the one hand,
(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}(g^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}\cong(\delta^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}
. On the other hand, by
?THM?
(
1
)
,
(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}(f^{\prime})^{!}\cong\widetilde{f}^{!}\gamma^{*}
, and hence
(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}(f^{\prime})^{!}g^{*}\cong\widetilde{f}^{!}\delta^{*}
.

Again by
?THM?
(
1
)
,
\widetilde{f}^{!}\delta^{*}\cong(\delta^{\prime})^{*}f^{!}
, and hence we obtain the desired base change isomorphism.

Part (
2
). Consider diagram (
4.1.2
). Since
\gamma
is surjective, by
?THM?
, we only need to show that
\gamma^{*}g^{*}f_{*}\cong\gamma^{*}f^{\prime}_{*}(g^{\prime})^{*}.

On the one hand,
\gamma^{*}g^{*}f_{*}\cong\delta^{*}f_{*}
.
On the other hand,
\gamma^{*}f^{\prime}_{*}\cong\widetilde{f}_{*}(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}
by
?THM?
(
2
)
. Then

\begin{split}\gamma^{*}f^{\prime}_{*}(g^{\prime})^{*}&\cong\widetilde{f}_{*}(\gamma^{\prime})^{*}(g^{\prime})^{*},\\
&\cong\widetilde{f}_{*}(\delta^{\prime})^{*}.\end{split}

Finally,
\widetilde{f}_{*}(\delta^{\prime})^{*}\cong\delta^{*}f_{*}
again by
?THM?
(
2
)
.
∎

Remark 4.11
.

The results above are also easily generalized to the case where we have a pro-smooth surjection
Z\rightarrow\mathscr{Z}
from a qcqs scheme
Z
, and
\mathscr{Y}\times_{\mathscr{Z}}Z\rightarrow Z
is pro-smooth (and
f
is representable of finite presentation).

4.1.3.
Excision

We need the following easy excision property of
\mathrm{Shv}
:

Proposition 4.12
.

Let
j:U\rightarrow X
be a quasi-compact open embedding of stacks with a closed immersion of complement
i:Z\rightarrow X
(i.e.,
U,Z
form a stratification of
X
after a base change to qcqs schemes). Assume that
i:Z\to X
is of finite presentation, we have the following:

(1)

The functor
i_{!}:\mathrm{Shv}(Z)\to\mathrm{Shv}(X)
is fully faithful, with essential image consisting of
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
, such that
j^{*}\mathcal{F}\cong 0
;

(2)

The sequence

(4.8)

j_{!}j^{*}\mathcal{F}\rightarrow\mathcal{F}\rightarrow i_{!}i^{*}\mathcal{F}

is a fiber sequence for every
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
.

Proof.

We first prove
i_{!}
is fully faithful. This is equivalent to proving
i^{*}i_{*}\cong\mathrm{id}
. Using Yoneda lemma and the fact that
\Hom_{\mathscr{C}}(x,y)=\varprojlim_{i}\Hom_{\mathscr{C}_{i}}(x_{i},y_{i})
whenever one has
\mathscr{C}=\varprojlim_{i}\mathscr{C}_{i}\in\mathrm{LinCat}_{k}
and
x_{i},y_{i}\in\mathscr{C}_{i}
are the images of
x,y\in\mathscr{C}
, one can reduce to the case that
X
is a qcqs scheme. By continuity of
i^{*}i_{*}
in this case, one only needs to prove
i^{*}i_{*}\mathcal{F}\cong\mathcal{F}
for
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{c}(Z)
coming from a finite type scheme, in which case the statement is well-known.

Then it is clear that (
2
) implies (
1
). To prove (
2
), the same argument as above helps us reduce to the case that
X
is a finite type scheme, in which the statement is well-known.

∎

4.1.4.
Cotruncated sheaves

For a general stack
X
,
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
is too large. One main cause is that we use the right Kan extension to extend the sheaf theory from qcqs schemes to all stacks, while in applications, we only care about sheaves enjoying certain finiteness properties concerning a specific presentation of
X
. In this section, we introduce the cotruncated sheaves to single out the sheaves that we care about.

This section is not essentially used in this article. One can avoid introducing this notion by working with Schubert cells of Hecke stacks instead of the entire Hecke stack in
§4.3.1
. We introduce this notion to compare with the sheaf theory in
[
4
]
(see
?THM?
) and simplify the notation in
§4.3.1
.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
X
be a stack. We define a
cotruncated presentation
of
X
to be the following data

(1)

a
\mathbb{Z}
-indexed (for simplicity) ind-system
\{X_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\in\Ind\!\mathrm{Stack}
,

(2)

an equivalence
X\cong\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
in
\mathrm{Stack}
,

such that each transition map
i_{n}:X_{n}\hookrightarrow X_{n+1}
is a closed immersion of finite presentation.

By
?THM?
(
1
)
, each
i_{n,!}:\mathrm{Shv}(X_{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{Shv}(X)
is fully faithful.

Remark 4.14
.

Note that
\mathrm{Shv}(X)\cong\varprojlim_{n}\mathrm{Shv}(X_{n})
with transition maps given by
i^{*}_{n}
, again by
[
23
, Proposition 6.2.1.9]
. Contrary to the case in
?THM?
, the transition maps
i^{*}_{n}
do not have a continuous left adjoint, so we can not rewrite
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
as a colimit. Therefore, objects in
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
in general do not enjoy the finiteness property coming from
\mathrm{Shv}(X_{n})
, unlike its dual counterpart
!
-sheaf theory (considered for example in
[
3
, §F]
).

Definition 4.15
.

We define the category of
cotruncated sheaves
on
X
(associated to the cotruncated presentation
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
) to be

\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(X):=\varinjlim_{n}\mathrm{Shv}(X_{n})

where the transition maps are given by
i_{n,*}
.

Remark 4.16
.

(1)

\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(X)
depends on the choice of a cotruncated presentation of
X
. In applications, we choose specific cotruncated representations of the spaces we study.

(2)

There is a fully faithful embedding
\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(X)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Shv}(X)
, and we can view
\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(X)
as a subcategory of
\mathrm{Shv}(X)
.

(3)

If
X
is an ind-scheme and
X\cong\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
with
X_{n}
of finite type and transition maps that are closed embeddings (sometimes called strict ind-schemes), then
\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(X)
is isomorphic to
\mathrm{Shv}^{!}(X):=\varprojlim_{n}\mathrm{Shv}(X_{n})
, where transition maps are given by
i_{n}^{!}
.

Example 4.17
.

Let
G
be a split reductive group acting on an affine smooth scheme
X
. Consider the loop stack (with cotruncated presentation)
LX/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)=\varinjlim_{n}L^{\geq-n}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)
as defined in
§4.3.1
. It is easy to see that all the transition maps
i_{n}:L^{\geq-n}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)\to L^{\geq-n-1}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)
are of finite presentation. Then
\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(LX/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))=\varinjlim_{n}\mathrm{Shv}(L^{\geq-n}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))
with transition maps
*
-push. We also have
\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(LX/L^{+}G)=\varinjlim_{n}\mathrm{Shv}(L^{\geq-n}X/L^{+}G)
. Therefore, the full subcategory
\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(LX/L^{+}G)\subset\mathrm{Shv}(LX/L^{+}G)
is the same as the large category of safe
*
-sheaves
\mathrm{SHV}_{s}^{*}(X_{F}/G_{\mathcal{O}})
defined in
[
4
, §7.2.3, §B.7]
.

Definition 4.18
.

Let
X
be a stack with cotruncated presentation
X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}
and
P
be a property of morphisms (of qcqs schemes) stable under base change such that all closed immersions of finite presentation satisfy
P
. Let
Y
be a stack. We say
f:X\rightarrow Y
is
ind-
P
, if each induced morphism
f_{n}:X_{n}\to Y
has property
P
.

Remark 4.19
.

Our definition of being ind-
P
depends on a cotruncated presentation of the source stack.

Example 4.20
.

If
f:X=\varinjlim_{n}X_{n}\rightarrow Y
is ind-fp, then the induced maps
f_{n}
give

f_{n,!}:\mathrm{Shv}(X_{n})\rightarrow\mathrm{Shv}(Y)

and we obtain a functor

f_{!}:\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(X)\rightarrow\mathrm{Shv}(Y)

by passing to colimit. Moreover, if
f
is ind-proper, we have a canonical isomorphism
f_{!}\cong f_{*}:\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(X)\to\mathrm{Shv}(Y)
.

4.1.5.
Derived fundamental class

In this section, we temporarily work with derived algebraic geometry. Since the derived aspect will be used only in
§5.1
in a mild way, we do not recall this theory and refer the readers to the literature (e.g.
[
29
]
,
[
20
]
, etc.).

Definition 4.21

(Derived fundamental class)
.

Consider a square of derived stacks which is Cartesian on the classical truncation

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b}

where
C,D
are derived Artin stacks and
b
is (representable) quasi-smooth of relative dimension
d
. Then there exists a natural map

(4.9)

[a]:\underline{k}_{A}\to a^{!}\underline{k}_{B}\langle-2d\rangle

defined as the composition

(4.10)

\underline{k}_{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}f^{*}\underline{k}_{C}\xrightarrow{[b]}f^{*}b^{!}\underline{k}_{D}\langle-2d\rangle\to a^{!}g^{*}\underline{k}_{D}\langle-2d\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}a^{!}\underline{k}_{B}\langle-2d\rangle

Where
[b]:=[C/D]
is the purity transformation for quasi-smooth maps between derived Artin stacks defined in
[
17
, Remark 3.8]
.

In this case, we say that the map
a:A\to B
is
quasi-smooth
, and we call the map (
4.9
) the
derived fundamental class
of the quasi-smooth map
a
. When
A
,
B
themselves are derived Artin stacks, it follows from
[
17
, Theorem 3.13]
that this notion of quasi-smoothness and derived fundamental class coincides with the usual one in
loc.cit
.

Remark 4.22
.

The condition that
b
is representable is not necessary.

Remark 4.23
.

We do not try to address the question concerning the dependence of the bottom map
b:C\to D
in the construction of
[a]
and the notion of quasi-smoothness. Whenever we use
?THM?
for non-Artin stacks, the choice of the bottom map
b
will be specified or clear from the context.

Lemma 4.24
.

In the situation of
?THM?
, suppose we have a commutative diagram of Artin stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q}

where
p
,
q
are smooth. Then the map

[a]:\underline{k}_{A}\to a^{!}\underline{k}_{B}\langle-2d\rangle

is an isomorphism. Here
d
is the relative dimension of
a
. Moreover, for any
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(S)
, we have a canonical isomorphism

[a]:p^{*}\mathcal{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}a^{!}q^{*}\mathcal{F}\langle-2d\rangle.

Proof.

This follows from

p^{*}\mathcal{F}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}p^{!}\mathcal{F}\langle-2\dim(p)\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}a^{!}q^{!}\mathcal{F}\langle-2\dim(p)\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}a^{!}q^{*}\mathcal{F}\langle-2d\rangle

and the construction of
[a]
.
∎

4.1.6.

We do not spell out all of them, but only give one typical example. The proof of other compatibilities will be the same as the following one:

Suppose
f
,

are representable of finite presentation, then we have the following commutative diagram

(4.12)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{*}b^{*}g_{!}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{!}a_{*}a^{*}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces b_{*}f_{!}a^{*}}

Proof.

Recall the natural base change map
g_{!}a_{*}\to b_{*}f_{!}
used in the bottom of (
4.12
) is the Beck-Chevalley base change map induced from the proper base change map
b^{*}g_{!}\cong f_{!}a^{*}
, which is defined as the composition

(4.13)

g_{!}a_{*}\to b_{*}b^{*}g_{!}a_{*}\cong b_{*}f_{!}a^{*}a_{*}\to b_{*}f_{!}.

Then the commutativity of (
4.12
) following from the tautological commutativity of the following diagram

\hbox to294.33pt{\vbox to50.42pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 147.16084pt\lower-25.20836pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-147.16084pt}{-19.6042pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -203.63 -27.13)} \pgfsys@hbox{62}\lxSVG@closescope }}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}}

{

∎

Remark 4.26
.

When
b
is D-proper in the sense of
[
15
, Definition 4.6.1(b)]
(hence
a
is D-proper as well), besides the Beck-Chevalley base change map
g_{!}a_{*}\to b_{*}f_{!}
defined in (
4.13
), we also have another natural map defined as the composition
g_{!}a_{*}\cong g_{!}a_{!}\cong b_{!}f_{!}\cong b_{*}f_{!}
. One can easily check that these two natural maps are homotopic to each other: Using the inductive definition of D-properness in
[
15
, Definition 4.6.1(b)]
and the definition of natural isomorphisms
b_{!}\cong b_{*}
and
a_{!}\cong a_{*}
in
[
15
, Definition 4.6.4(ii)]
, one can reduce to the case where
a
,
b
are isomorphisms, and then the statement is trivial.

4.2.
Cohomological correspondence with kernel

In this section, we briefly review the notion of cohomological correspondence used in
[
10
]
and many other places. Compared to
[
10
]
, we only consider Cartesian pull-back functoriality, but we need to put a kernel in the cohomological correspondence and work over non-Artin stacks. This is a mild generalization given
§4.1
, and readers familiar with the cohomological correspondences in
[
10
]
can safely skip this part.

4.2.1.
Pushable squares

In this section, we briefly review the notion of pushable squares introduced in
[
10
, §3.1]
.

A square of stacks

(4.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

is called
pushable
if the map
a:A\to\widetilde{B}
in the following diagram is proper

(4.16)

g^{*}f_{!}\to f^{\prime}_{!}g^{\prime*}

via

g^{*}f_{!}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\widetilde{f}_{!}\widetilde{g}^{*}\to\widetilde{f}_{!}a_{*}a^{*}\widetilde{g}^{*}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}f^{\prime}_{!}g^{\prime*}.

Proof.

When all the stacks involved are Artin stacks, this is
[
10
, Proposition 3.2.3&Proposition 3.2.4]
. The proof in
loc.cit
carries to general case given
?THM?
.
∎

4.2.2.
Definition

Consider a correspondence between stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

where
g
is of finite presentation.

Definition 4.28
.

For any
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
,
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{Shv}(Y)
and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
, a
cohomological correspondence (of degree
d
) between
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathcal{G}
(supported on
C
with kernel
\mathcal{K}
)
is an element

c\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle):=\Hom^{0}(g_{!}(f^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K}),\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Hom^{0}(f^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K},g^{!}\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle).

Remark 4.29
.

In our application, we also need the case where
C
carries a cotruncated presentation (
?THM?
) such that
g
is ind-fp and
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(C)
. All results in
§4.2
can be generalized to this case without difficulty.

Example 4.30

(Tautological correspondence)
.

Given a sheaf
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
, we have a tautological element

\taut_{f}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{Y,\underline{k}_{Y}}(\mathcal{F},f^{*}\mathcal{F}).

When
f
has the form of a projection
f:Y=X\times Z\to X
, we also write
\taut_{Z}:=\taut_{f}
.

Example 4.31

(Fundamental class)
.

Consider correspondence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}

where
g
is smooth of dimension
d_{g}
.
Given a sheaf
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(Y)
, we have a element

[g]\in\mathrm{Corr}_{X,\underline{k}_{X}}(g^{*}\mathcal{G},\mathcal{G}\langle-2d_{g}\rangle)

given by the relative fundamental class
[g]:g^{*}\mathcal{F}\to g^{!}\mathcal{F}\langle-2d_{g}\rangle
in
?THM?
. When
g
has the form of a projection
g:Y\times Z\to Y
, we also write
[Z]:=[g]
.

Moreover, assume we are given
\mathcal{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
and
\widetilde{K}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\widetilde{C})
equipped with a morphism

(4.18)

\alpha:q^{*}\mathcal{K}\to\widetilde{K}.

We first introduce pull-back functoriality as defined in
[
10
, Section 4.4]
. While the definition in
[
10
]
applies whenever the left square is
pullable
, we only need the case that the special case that the left square is Cartesian.
6
6

6

While the pull-back functoriality along pullable maps of correspondences in
[
10
, Section 4.4]
is subtle, the Cartesian pull-back functoriality is trivial.

Definition 4.32
.

When the left-square in (
4.17
) is Cartesian and the map
\alpha
in (
4.18
) is an isomorphism, we say that
q
is
Cartesian pullable
. In this case, for each cohomological correspondence

c\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)

where
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X),\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{Shv}(Y)
, we define its pull-back along
q
to be the element

q^{*}(c)\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\widetilde{C},q^{*}\mathcal{K}}(q_{X}^{*}\mathcal{F},q_{Y}^{*}\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)

as the image of
c
along the map

\begin{split}\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)&=\Hom^{0}(g_{!}(f^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K}),\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)\\
&\to\Hom^{0}(q_{Y}^{*}g_{!}(f^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K}),q_{Y}^{*}\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Hom^{0}(\widetilde{g}_{!}q^{*}(f^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{K}),q_{Y}^{*}\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Hom^{0}(\widetilde{g}_{!}(\widetilde{f}^{*}q_{X}^{*}\mathcal{F}\otimes q^{*}\mathcal{K}),q_{Y}^{*}\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)\\
&=\mathrm{Corr}_{\widetilde{C},q^{*}\mathcal{K}}(q_{X}^{*}\mathcal{F},q_{Y}^{*}\mathcal{G}\langle d\rangle)\end{split}.

Now we introduce push-forward functoriality as defined in
[
10
, Section 4.3]
.

Definition 4.33
.

When the right square in (
4.17
) is pushable in the sense of
§4.2.1
and the vertical maps are fp, we say that
q
is pushable. In this case, we define the
push-forward of cohomological correspondence
along
q
as follows: For each cohomological correspondence

c\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\widetilde{C},\widetilde{K}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\widetilde{X})
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\widetilde{Y})
, we define its push-forward along
q
to be the element

q_{!}(c)\in\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(q_{X,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},q_{Y,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)

defined as the image of
c
along the map

\begin{split}\mathrm{Corr}_{\widetilde{C},\widetilde{K}}(\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)&=\Hom^{0}(\widetilde{g}_{!}(\widetilde{f}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\widetilde{K}),\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)\\
&\to\Hom^{0}(q_{Y,!}\widetilde{g}_{!}(\widetilde{f}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\otimes q^{*}\mathcal{K}),q_{Y,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Hom^{0}(g_{!}q_{!}(\widetilde{f}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\otimes q^{*}\mathcal{K}),q_{Y,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Hom^{0}(g_{!}(q_{!}\widetilde{f}^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mathcal{K}),q_{Y,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)\\
&\to\Hom^{0}(g_{!}(f^{*}q_{X,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}\otimes\mathcal{K}),q_{Y,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)\\
&=\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}}(q_{X,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},q_{Y,!}\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}\langle d\rangle)\end{split}

where the fifth map uses the base change for pushable square (
4.16
).

4.2.4.
Composition

Consider the following diagram of correspondences of stacks with leftwards maps fp

(4.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}

where the square is Cartesian. Given
\mathcal{K}_{C}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C)
,
\mathcal{K}_{D}\in\mathrm{Shv}(D)
,
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X)
,
\mathcal{G}\in\mathrm{Shv}(Y)
,
\mathcal{H}\in\mathrm{Shv}(Z)
. Define
\mathcal{K}_{E}:=g^{\prime*}\mathcal{K}_{D}\otimes h^{\prime*}\mathcal{K}_{C}
. For every
d_{1},d_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is an obviously defined map

(4.20)

\mathrm{Corr}_{D,\mathcal{K}_{D}}(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{H}\langle d_{2}\rangle)\otimes\mathrm{Corr}_{C,\mathcal{K}_{C}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{G}\langle d_{1}\rangle)\to\mathrm{Corr}_{E,\mathcal{K}_{E}}(\mathcal{F},\mathcal{H}\langle d_{1}+d_{2}\rangle)

which would be denoted

(4.21)

c_{2}\otimes c_{1}\mapsto c_{2}\circ c_{1}.

This is what we call the composition of cohomological correspondences.

4.2.5.
Compatibilities

In this section, we state all the compatibilities between the three operations on cohomological correspondences: push-forward
?THM?
, pull-back
?THM?
, and composition in
§4.2.4
. There is a compatibility for each pair of the three operations above, which gives us six different compatibilities. All of them except the base change compatibility between push and pull are easy to state and prove given
?THM?
. We only state the base change compatibility and leave the routine proof to the reader.

Proposition 4.34
.

Suppose that we have a commutative square of correspondences of stacks

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{C_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y,42}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y,43}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{C,42}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{C,43}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X,42}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X,43}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y,21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{C,21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X,21}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{Y,31}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{C,31}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{X,31}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}

such that all three left and right faces of the cubes are Cartesian squares and
g_{i}
are of finite presentation for all
i
. Given any

•

\mathcal{F}_{3}\in\mathrm{Shv}(X_{3})
,
\mathcal{G}_{3}\in\mathrm{Shv}(Y_{3})
,
\mathcal{K}_{1}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C_{1})

\mathcal{K}_{3}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C_{3})
,

•

\alpha_{31}:p_{C,31}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}\to\mathcal{K}_{3}
,

•

integer
d
,

we set

•

\mathcal{F}_{1}=p_{X,31,!}\mathcal{F}_{3}
,
\mathcal{F}_{4}=p_{X,43}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{3}
,
\mathcal{F}_{2}=p_{X,21}^{*}\mathcal{F}_{1}\cong p_{X,42,!}\mathcal{F}_{4}
and similarly for
\mathcal{G}_{i}
;

•

\mathcal{K}_{2}=p_{C,21}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{1}
and
\mathcal{K}_{4}=p_{C,43}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{3}
;

•

\alpha_{42}=p_{C,43}^{*}\alpha_{31}:p_{C,42}^{*}\mathcal{K}_{2}\to\mathcal{K}_{4}
.

If
p_{C,31}
and
p_{C,42}
are pushable as in
?THM?
, and
p_{C,21}
and
p_{C,43}
are Cartesian pullable as in
?THM?
, then we have an identity

p_{C,21}^{*}\circ p_{C,31,!}=p_{C,42,!}\circ p_{C,43}^{*}:\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{3},\mathcal{K}_{3}}(\mathcal{F}_{3},\mathcal{G}_{3}\langle d\rangle)\to\mathrm{Corr}_{C_{2},\mathcal{K}_{2}}(\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathcal{G}_{2}\langle d\rangle).

Proof.

The only slightly non-obvious part is provided by
?THM?
.
∎

4.3.
Geometric setup

We now introduce the geometric objects involved in this article. We remind the reader again that all the stacks in this section will be non-derived. Experienced readers can skip this part and come back for notations (see
§4.3.5
for a summary of notations).

Warning 4.35
.

To simplify the notations, we do not include the half-spin twist as in
[
4
, §10]
in the definition of various moduli spaces. This makes all our constructions different from the normalization in
loc.cit
unless the
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-action on
X
is trivial. One can easily adapt these normalizations.

4.3.1.
Moduli spaces

Throughout this section, we fix an affine smooth
G
-variety
X=\Spec\!A
together with a closed embedding
X\subset V
where
V
is a finite-dimensional representation of
G
regarded as a
G
-variety.

We start from the local theory. We use
L^{+}X\in\mathrm{Sch}_{\mathrm{qcqs}}
to denote the jet space of
X
which is defined as

L^{+}X:\mathrm{Aff}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Spc}

L^{+}X(R)=X(R[[t]]).

We use
LX\in\mathrm{Stack}
to denote the loop space defined as

LX:\mathrm{Aff}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Spc}

LX(R)=X(R(\!(t)\!)).

The loop space
LX
carries a natural cotruncated presentation
LX=\varinjlim_{n}L^{\geq-n}X
where the closed subfunctor
L^{\geq-n}X\subset LX
is defined by requiring the images of degree one generators of
\mathcal{O}(V)
under the map
\mathcal{O}(V)\to A\to R(\!(t)\!)
factor through
t^{-n}R[[t]]\subset R(\!(t)\!)
.

When we take
X=G
, the construction above gives us the jet group scheme
L^{+}G
and loop group
LG
.

We use
\Aut(D)\in\mathrm{Sch}
to denote the group scheme of automorphisms of the formal disk
D=\Spec\!\mathbb{F}_{q}[[t]]
preserving the center of the disc, i.e., those automorphisms which are
\mathrm{id}
mod
t
. We have
\Aut(D)=\Aut(D)^{\mathrm{u}}\rtimes\mathbb{G}_{m}
, where
\Aut(D)^{\mathrm{u}}
is the pro-unipotent radical of
\Aut(D)
. There is a natural action of
\Aut(D)
on
L^{+}X
and
LX
.

Throughout this section, we fix a geometrically connected smooth projective curve
C\in\mathrm{Sch}
. There is a natural map of stacks
\mathrm{Coor}:C\to B\!\Aut(D)
given by the canonical étale
\Aut(D)
-torsor of local coordinates of
C
.

Now we come to the semi-local version of the constructions above. Following
[
16
]
(See also
[
27
, §2]
), we now define the jet and loop spaces over the curve
C
. For each finite set
I
, we denote the (multi)jet scheme of
X
over
C^{I}
by
(L^{+}X)_{C^{I}}\in\mathrm{Sch}
whose
S
-points for
S\in\mathrm{Aff}
are given by

(L^{+}X)_{C^{I}}(S)=\{c_{I}\in C^{I}(S)\penalty\ \textup{and}\penalty\ D_{c_{I}}\to X\},

where
D_{c_{I}}
is the formal completion of
C\times S
along the graph
\Gamma_{c_{I}}
of
c_{I}
. We use
l_{\mathrm{sl},I}:(L^{+}X)_{C^{I}}\to C^{I}
to denote the map remembering the legs.

We denote the (multi)loop space of
X
over
C^{I}
by
(LX)_{C^{I}}\in\mathrm{Stack}
whose
S
-points for
S\in\mathrm{Aff}
are given by

(LX)_{C^{I}}(S)=\{c_{I}\in C^{I}(S)\penalty\ \textup{and}\penalty\ \mathring{D}_{c_{I}}\to X\},

where
\mathring{D}_{c_{I}}
is the punctured disc along the graph
\Gamma_{c_{I}}
of
c_{I}
defined in
[
27
, §2.6]
. We use
l_{\mathrm{sl},I}:(LX)_{C^{I}}\to C^{I}
to denote the map remembering the legs.

For each subset
J\subset I
we also consider the closed substack
(LX)_{C^{J\subset I}}\subset(LX)_{C^{I}}
defined by

(LX)_{C^{J\subset I}}(S)=\{c_{I}\in C^{I}(S)\penalty\ \textup{and}\penalty\ D_{c_{I}}\backslash\Gamma_{c_{J}}\to X\}.

Finally, we define moduli spaces involved in global theory. We use
\Bun_{G}\in\mathrm{Stack}
to denote the moduli of
G
-bundles on the curve
C
and
\Bun_{G}^{X}\in\mathrm{Stack}
to denote the moduli of
G
-bundles on
C
together with a regular
X
-section. More specifically, the
S
-points of
\Bun_{G}^{X}
for
S\in\mathrm{Aff}
is given by

\Bun_{G}^{X}(S)=\{p:\mathcal{E}\to C\times S\penalty\ \textup{an \'{e}tale $G$-torsor on $C\times S$ and}\penalty\ s:C\times S\to X\times^{G}\mathcal{E}\penalty\ \textup{a section of $p$}\}.

For each finite set
I
, we use
\Bun_{G,I}^{X}\in\mathrm{Stack}
to denote the moduli of
G
-bundles with a rational
X
-section which is regular away from legs indexed by
I
. Precisely, for each
S\in\mathrm{Aff}
, we define the
S
-points of
\Bun_{G,I}^{X}
to be

\Bun_{G,I}^{X}(S)=\{(c_{I},\mathcal{E},s)|c_{I}\in C^{I}(S),\mathcal{E}\in\Bun_{G}(S),s\penalty\ \textup{is a section of $X\times^{G}\mathcal{E}$ over $C\times S\backslash\Gamma_{c_{I}}$}\}.

Finally, for each subset
J\subset I
, we also have
\Bun^{X}_{G,J\subset I}
defined via the fiber product

and

. We use

rather than

to denote the fiber product

More explicitly, the stack
\hk_{G}^{\mathring{X},\mathrm{l}}
is involved in the following correspondence:

(4.22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hk_{G}^{\mathring{X},\mathrm{l}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LX\times^{L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}LG\rtimes\Aut(D)/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LX/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LX/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}}

where
\overrightarrow{h}^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}(x,g)=x
and
\overleftarrow{h}^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}(x,g)=xg
for
x\in LX
and
g\in LG\rtimes\Aut(D)
.

For the finite set
I=[r]
, we define the local iterated Hecke stack to be

\widetilde{\hk}^{\mathrm{l}}_{G,I}:=\hk^{\mathrm{l}}_{G}\times_{B(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}\cdots\times_{B(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))}\hk^{\mathrm{l}}_{G}.
