Then we see that

(5.1)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathring{X},\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{V}}(\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle d\rangle)\\
=&\Hom^{0}(\IC_{V}*\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle d\rangle)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}&\Hom^{0}(\IC_{V}*i_{\mathrm{l},*}\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)},i_{\mathrm{l},*}\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d\rangle)\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}&\Hom^{0}(\IC_{V},\overleftarrow{h}^{+,X,!}_{\mathrm{l}}\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d\rangle)\\
=&\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{X,\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{V}}(\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)},\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d\rangle)\end{split}.

5.1.2.
Construction

Now we assume
V\in\Rep(\check{G})^{\heartsuit,\omega}
is
irreducible minuscule
with higher weight
\lambda_{V}
. More precisely, we assume that
\IC_{V}
is
the
(shifted) constant sheaf supported on the minuscule Schubert cell indexed by
\lambda_{V}
. For various versions of Hecke stacks, we add a subscript
V
(or the extreme weight
\lambda_{V}
) to indicate the Schubert cell supporting
\IC_{V}
. We can further rewrite the vector space in (
5.1
) as

(5.2)

\begin{split}&\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathring{X},\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{V}}(\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle d\rangle)\\
=&\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{X,\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{V}}(\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)},\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d\rangle)\\
=&\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{X,\mathrm{l}}_{G,V},\underline{k}}(\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)},\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d-d_{G,\lambda_{V}}\rangle)\end{split}

where the number
d_{G,\lambda_{V}}=\langle 2\rho_{G},\lambda_{V}\rangle
.

To define the derived fundamental class, we make the following assumption:

Assumption 5.1
.

There exists a split reductive subgroup
H\subset G
and
Y\in\Rep(H)^{\heartsuit,\omega}
such that
X=Y\times^{H}G
.

Now we choose a minuscule coweight
\lambda\in X_{*}(T_{H})
such that
V
has highest weight
\lambda_{V}=\lambda^{+}
. We refer to
§2.5
for our notations concerning Lie theory.

Definition 5.2
.

For
\lambda\in X_{*}(T_{H})
and
Y\in\Rep(H)^{\heartsuit,\omega}
, we define the
defect module
of
\lambda
with respect to
Y
to be

D_{\lambda,Y}:=Y[[t]]\cdot t^{-\lambda}/(Y[[t]]\cap Y[[t]]\cdot t^{-\lambda})\in\Rep(\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D))

and the
defect
of
\lambda
with respect to
Y
to be

\delta_{\lambda,Y}:=\dim(D_{\lambda,Y}).

Here

(5.3)

\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}:=t^{\lambda}L^{+}Ht^{-\lambda}\cap L^{+}H

is a parahoric subgroup of
L^{+}H
.

We define
V_{H,\lambda}\in\Rep(\check{H})^{\heartsuit,\omega}
to be the unique irreducible representation of
\check{H}
containing extremal weight
\lambda
.

Now, we are going to construct an element

c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{V}}(\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle d_{V,\lambda}\rangle)

where

d_{V,\lambda}:=d_{G,\lambda}-2d_{H,\lambda}+2\delta_{\lambda,Y}.

Consider diagram

(5.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hk^{X,\mathrm{l}}_{G,V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{\mathrm{l}}^{\hk}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{+,X}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}^{+,X}_{\mathrm{l}}}

where left and right vertical arrows are isomorphisms. It is clear that both squares are pushable in the sense of
§4.2.1
and the push-forward functoriality of cohomological correspondences
?THM?
gives us a map

(5.5)

\begin{split}a_{\mathrm{l},!}^{\hk}:&\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda},\underline{k}}(\underline{k}_{L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)},\underline{k}_{L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d-d_{G,\lambda}\rangle)\\
\to&\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{X,\mathrm{l}}_{G,V},\underline{k}}(\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)},\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d-d_{G,\lambda}\rangle).\end{split}

Define

(5.6)

\mathcal{P}_{G,\lambda}=t^{\lambda}L^{+}Gt^{-\lambda}\cap L^{+}G.

Consider the correspondence diagram

(5.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY/\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY\times^{L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}L^{+}Ht^{\lambda}L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces LY/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hk^{\mathring{Y},\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{Y}_{H,\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}^{Y}_{H,\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{Y}_{H,\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}^{Y}_{H,\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the lower half diagram is exactly (
4.22
) for
H
restricted to the
\lambda
-Schubert cell. The isomorphism
s
can be described as follows: One first defines the map

(5.8)

\widetilde{s}:LY\to LY\times(L^{+}Ht^{\lambda}L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D))

as

(5.9)

\widetilde{s}(y)=(y,(t^{\lambda},1)).

and defines

(5.10)

s_{\mathcal{P}}:\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D)\to(L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D))\times(L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D))

(5.11)

s_{\mathcal{P}}(p,a)=((p,a),(t^{-\lambda}pa^{-1}(t)^{\lambda},a)).

Then one checks that the map
\widetilde{s}
is
s_{\mathcal{P}}
-equivariant and descends to a map
s
, where we remind the reader that the right action of
\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D)
on
LY
is obtained by restricting the right action of
L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)
on
LY
given by

(5.12)

y(t)\cdot(h(t),a)=y(a(t))h(a(t))\in LY

for
y(t)\in LY
and
(h(t),a)\in L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)
, and the right action of
(L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D))^{2}
on
LY\times(LH\rtimes\Aut(D))
is given by

(5.13)

(y,(h,a))\cdot((h_{1},a_{1}),(h_{2},a_{2}))=(y\cdot(h_{1},a_{1}),(h_{1},a_{1})^{-1}\cdot(h,a)\cdot(h_{2},a_{2}))\in LY\times LH\rtimes\Aut(D)

for
(y,(h,a))\in LY\times(LH\rtimes\Aut(D))
and
((h_{1},a_{1}),(h_{2},a_{2}))\in(L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D))^{2}
.

Under the isomorphism
s
, we can rewrite the upper row of (
5.4
) as the top of the diagram

(5.14)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}Y\cap L^{+}Y\cdot t^{-\lambda}/\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{+}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overrightarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}}

such that for

y\in L^{+}Y\cap L^{+}Y\cdot t^{-\lambda}

a representative of an element in
L^{+}Y\cap L^{+}Y\cdot t^{-\lambda}/\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D)
, we have

\overrightarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}(y)=y\in L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)

\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}(y)=y\cdot t^{\lambda}\in L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D).

Now, it is easy to see that the map
\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}
is quasi-smooth of relative virtual dimension
d_{H,\lambda}-\delta_{\lambda,Y}
. In fact, one can choose suitable
n,m,k\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 1}
depending only on
(H,Y,\lambda)
such that
one can define

L^{(m)}H:=L^{+}H/L^{\geq m}H

\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}^{(m)}=\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}/L^{\geq m}H

\Aut^{(k)}(D)=\Aut(D)/\Aut^{\geq k}(D)

L^{(n)}Y:=L^{+}Y/t^{n}L^{+}Y.

and get a Cartesian diagram

(5.15)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces L^{(n)}Y/L^{(m)}H\rtimes\Aut^{(k)}(D)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda,\mathrm{fin}}:=L^{(n)}Y\cap L^{(n)}Y\cdot t^{\lambda}/\mathcal{P}_{H,\lambda}^{(m)}\rtimes\Aut^{(k)}(D)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\mathrm{l}}^{\hk}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\llcorner}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{fin},\mathrm{l}}}

where the bottom map is quasi-smooth, hence makes
\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}
a quasi-smooth map in the sense of
?THM?
. Then the construction in
loc.cit
gives a derived fundamental class

(5.16)

\begin{split}[\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}]&=[\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda}/(L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D))]\\
&\in\Hom^{0}(\underline{k}_{\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda}},\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y,!}_{\mathrm{fin},\mathrm{l}}\underline{k}_{L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle-2d_{H,\lambda}+2\delta_{\lambda,Y}\rangle)\\
&=\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{Y,\mathrm{l}}_{H,\lambda},\underline{k}}(\underline{k}_{L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)},\underline{k}_{L^{+}Y/L^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d_{V,\lambda}-d_{G,\lambda}\rangle)\end{split}.

We can finally define

(5.17)

\begin{split}c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}:=a_{\mathrm{l},!}^{\hk}[\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}]&\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{X,\mathrm{l}}_{G,V},\underline{k}}(\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)},\underline{k}_{L^{+}X/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)}\langle d_{V,\lambda}-d_{G,\lambda}\rangle)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathring{X},\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{V}}(\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle d_{V,\lambda}\rangle)\end{split}

where the map
a_{\mathrm{l},!}^{\hk}
is the map (
5.5
). This finishes the definition of local special cohomological correspondences via the derived fundamental class.

5.1.3.
Pull-back to global moduli

In this section, we restrict to the case that
X
is
G
-homogeneous. In this case, we have
X=H\backslash G
for a split reductive subgroup
H\subset G
, and we have
\Bun_{G}^{X}=\Bun_{H}
. In this case, we have defect
\delta_{\lambda,Y}=0
and
d_{V,\lambda}=d_{G,\lambda}-2d_{H,\lambda}
.
We now describe the global cohomological correspondences
c_{V,\lambda}
constructed in (
4.39
) given the input
c^{\mathrm{l}}_{V,\lambda}
constructed in (
5.17
) (i.e. consider the local datum
(\{1\},V,c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}})
in the language of
?THM?
and
?THM?
).

.

The push-forward functoriality in
?THM?
gives us a map

\begin{split}\pi_{\lambda,!}^{\hk}:\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk_{H,\lambda}^{\mathrm{g}},\underline{k}}(\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}},\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}}\langle-2-2d_{H,\lambda}\rangle)&\to\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk_{G,V}^{\mathrm{g}},\underline{k}}(\pi_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}},\pi_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{H}}\langle-2-2d_{H,\lambda}\rangle)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk_{G}^{\mathrm{g}},\IC_{V}}(\mathcal{P}_{X},\mathcal{P}_{X}\langle d_{V,\lambda}-2\rangle)\end{split}.

Then the element
c_{V,\lambda}
constructed in (
4.39
) from the local fundamental class
c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}
in (
5.17
) can be identified as

c_{V,\lambda}=\pi_{\lambda,!}^{\hk}[\hk_{H,\lambda}^{\mathrm{g}}/\Bun_{H}]

where
[\hk_{H,\lambda}^{\mathrm{g}}/\Bun_{H}]=[\overleftarrow{h}_{H,\mathrm{g}}]
is the fundamental class introduced in
?THM?
.

Proof.

?THM?
in which the middle square is given by

Consider
\pi_{!}^{\hk}:\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk_{G,V}^{X,\mathrm{g}},\underline{k}}(\delta_{X}^{\mathrm{g}},\delta_{X}^{\mathrm{g}}\langle-2-2d_{H,\lambda}\rangle)\to\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk_{G,V},\underline{k}}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C},\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C}\langle-2-2d_{H,\lambda}\rangle)
. Unwinding the definition of
c_{V,\lambda}
, we get

\begin{split}c_{V,\lambda}&=[C]\circ(\pi_{!}^{\hk}f_{G,\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{l}}^{X,\hk,*}a_{\mathrm{l},!}^{\hk}[\hk_{H,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}/BL^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)])\circ\taut_{C}\\
&=[C]\circ\pi_{!}^{\hk}a_{\mathrm{g},!}^{\hk}f_{H,\mathrm{g}\to\mathrm{l}}^{\hk,*}[\hk_{H,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}/BL^{+}H\rtimes\Aut(D)]\circ\taut_{C}\\
&=[C]\circ\pi_{!}^{\hk}a_{\mathrm{g},!}^{\hk}[\hk_{H,\lambda}^{\mathrm{g}}/\Bun_{H}\times C]\circ\taut_{C}\\
&=\pi_{\lambda,!}^{\hk}[\hk_{H,\lambda}^{\mathrm{g}}/\Bun_{H}]\end{split}

which concludes the proof.

∎

5.2.
Middle-dimensional correspondence

Assume we are under
?THM?
. In this section, we consider a specific case of
?THM?
that we care about the most: the middle-dimensional case, that is, the case
d_{V}+d_{W}=2
. We say that this case is middle-dimensional because when
|I|=2
, the associated special cycle classes are middle-dimensional Borel-Moore homology classes in this case.

In case
d_{V}+d_{W}=2
, we see that
c^{\mathrm{l}}_{V\otimes W}\in\Hom^{0}(V\otimes W,\mathrm{PL}_{X,\hbar})
. Note that there is a canonical element
c^{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{triv}}\in\Hom^{0}(\mathrm{triv},\mathrm{PL}_{X,\hbar})
where
\mathrm{triv}\in\Rep(\check{G})
is the trivial
\check{G}
-representation. To single out the most interesting example, we make the following assumptions:

Assumption 5.4

(Poisson-purity)
.

We have
d_{V}+d_{W}=2
, and there exists a bilinear form
b:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}

such that the map

c_{V\otimes W}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\Hom^{0}(V\otimes W,\mathrm{PL}_{X,\hbar})

is given by

(5.18)

V\otimes W\xrightarrow{b}\mathrm{triv}\xrightarrow{c^{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathrm{triv}}}\mathrm{PL}_{X,\hbar}.

Now we further make the following assumption:

Assumption 5.5

(Minuscule)
.

We have
V,W\in\Rep(\check{G})^{\heartsuit,\omega}
are irreducible minuscule representations of
\check{G}
. More specifically, we take
V,W\in\Rep(\check{G})^{\heartsuit}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Sat_{G}^{\heartsuit}
to be
the
(shift of) constant sheaf supported on the corresponding minuscule Schubert cell.

Remark 5.6
.

The minuscule assumption is only necessary when applying the construction in
§5.1
.

In this case, whenever a non-zero bilinear form
b:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}
exists, there would exist a canonical bilinear form

(5.19)

b_{\mathrm{geo}}:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}

(or more precisely, a map
\IC_{V}*\IC_{W}\to\IC_{\mathrm{triv}}
) coming from geometry which we are going to explain now.

Suppose
V
is of higher (minuscule) weight
\lambda_{V}\in X_{*}(T)^{+}
(hence
W
has lowest weight
-\lambda_{V}
). We use

(5.20)

P_{G,\lambda_{V}}

to denote the parabolic subgroup of
G
generated by roots
\{\alpha\in\Phi_{G}|\langle\alpha,\lambda_{V}\rangle\leq 0\}
(hence contains the opposite Borel). Then it is easy to see that
\Hom_{\Sat_{G}^{\heartsuit}}(\IC_{V}*\IC_{W},\IC_{\mathrm{triv}})=H^{0}(G/P_{G,\lambda_{V}},\underline{k})
. Therefore, we can take
b_{\mathrm{geo}}:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}
to be given by the canonical element
1\in H^{0}(G/P_{G,\lambda_{V}},\underline{k})
(the fundamental class of
G/P_{\mu}
under Poincare duality).

When our affine smooth
G
-variety
X
has the form
X=Y\times^{H}G
, for each
\lambda\in X_{*}(T_{H})
such that
\lambda^{+}=\lambda_{V}
we have the derived fundamental class
c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}
constructed in (
5.17
) and similarly for
\mu\in X_{*}(T_{H})
where
\mu^{+}=\lambda_{W}
a class
c^{\mathrm{l}}_{W,\mu}
. Assume
?THM?
is satisfied, we denote the bilinear form in
loc.cit
by

(5.21)

b_{\lambda,\mu}:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}.

Now, it is a natural question to ask about the relation between
b_{\lambda,\mu}
and
b_{\mathrm{geo}}
. Since the former
b_{\lambda,\mu}
involves the choice of the
G
-variety
X
as well as some choices of
\lambda,\mu\in X_{*}(T_{H})^{+}
, while the second
b_{\mathrm{geo}}
does not involve any extra choice, these two forms a priori have no relation and would differ by a scalar in general. We denote

(5.22)

b_{\lambda,\mu}=\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}\cdot b_{\mathrm{geo}}.

5.2.1.
Automorphic Clifford relations

We have the following reformulation of
?THM?
under the strong assumptions in the previous section, which is what we call the automorphic Clifford relation:

Corollary 5.7

(Automorphic Clifford relation)
.

Under
?THM?
. Suppose
?THM?
and
?THM?
hold. Consider the diagram

(5.23)

where
b_{\mathrm{g}}=\overleftarrow{h}_{\{1\},\mathrm{g}}
and the map
\Delta^{\hk}_{V}
is given by sending a modification
\mathcal{E}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
to the iterated modification
\mathcal{E}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{E}
.
We get a map

\begin{split}\Delta^{\hk}_{V,\mathrm{g},!}:\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathrm{g}}_{G,\{1\},V},\underline{k}}(\mathcal{P}_{X},\mathcal{P}_{X}\langle-2-2d_{G,\lambda_{V}}\rangle)&\to\mathrm{Corr}_{\widetilde{\hk}_{G,\{1,2\},V\boxtimes W}^{\mathrm{g}},\underline{k}}(\mathcal{P}_{X},\mathcal{P}_{X}\langle-2-2d_{G,\lambda_{V}}\rangle)\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk_{G,\{1,2\},V\boxtimes W}^{\mathrm{g}},\IC_{V\boxtimes W}}(\mathcal{P}_{X},\mathcal{P}_{X}\langle-2\rangle)\end{split}

via the push-forward functoriality in
?THM?
. Assuming
?THM?
, we have

c_{V\boxtimes W}-c_{W\boxtimes V}=\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}\cdot\Delta_{V,\mathrm{g},!}^{\hk}[\hk^{\mathrm{g}}_{G,\{1\},V}/\Bun_{G}],

where
c_{V\boxtimes W}
and
c_{W\boxtimes V}
are defined in (
4.39
) and
[\hk^{\mathrm{g}}_{G,\{1\},V}/\Bun_{G}]
is the (relative) fundamental class defined in
?THM?
viewed as a cohomological correspondence.

Proof.

This follows easily from the base change compatibility between pull-back and push-forward of cohomological correspondences in
?THM?
.
∎

5.2.2.
Examples

There are many examples that
?THM?
and
?THM?
hold.

Example 5.8

(Rankin–Selberg convolution: homogeneous case)
.

Case
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
and
X=\GL_{n}
. See the discussion in
§7
.

Example 5.9

(Rankin–Selberg convolution: inhomogeneous case)
.

Case
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
and
X=\GL_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
, on which the action in given by
(g,x)\cdot(g_{1},g_{2})=(g_{1}^{-1}gg_{2},x\cdot g_{2})
for
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
and
(g,x)\in\GL_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}
. One has
\check{M}=T^{*}(\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n})
and
?THM?
is proved in
[
7
]
. One can take
V=\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n}
and
W=V^{*}
, which uniquely determines the coweights
\lambda,\mu\in X_{*}(T_{H})
up to Weyl group translation. In this case,
?THM?
and
?THM?
are satisfied.

Example 5.10

(Orthogonal Gan–Gross–Prasad)
.

Case
G=\SO_{n}\times\SO_{n-1}
and
X=\SO_{n}
, on which the action in given by
x\cdot(g_{1},g_{2})=g_{1}^{-1}xg_{2}
for
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\SO_{n}\times\SO_{n-1}
and
x\in\SO_{n}
. One has
\check{M}=\mathrm{Std}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}
as a symplectic representation of
\check{G}
.
?THM?
in this case is proved in
[
8
]
. One can take
W=V=\mathrm{Std}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}
, which uniquely determines the coweights
\lambda,\mu\in X_{*}(T_{H})
up to Weyl group translation. In this case,
?THM?
and
?THM?
are satisfied.

Example 5.11

(Strongly tempered case)
.

Case
T^{*}X
is strongly tempered, the hyperspherical dual
\check{M}
is a symplectic representation of
\check{G}
. This includes
?THM?
,
?THM?
, and
?THM?
. In this case,
?THM?
predicts that one can take
V
and
W=V^{*}
to be constituents of
\check{M}
, and then
?THM?
will be satisfied. When
X=H\backslash G
is homogeneous, by checking all such examples in
[
31
]
, we find that for each irreducible
\check{G}
-representation
V
, the multiplicity of
V
in
\check{M}
is exactly the number of
\lambda\in X_{*}(T_{H})^{+}
such that
\lambda^{+}=\lambda_{V}
and
d_{V,\lambda}=1
. The representation
\check{M}
is multiplicity-free and has all its constituents minuscule in all such examples in
loc.cit
. Therefore, the construction of local special cohomological correspondences via
§5.1
should provide a
canonical
presentation of
\check{M}
as a direct sum of minuscule irreducible
\check{G}
-representations.

Example 5.12

(Linear period)
.

When
G=\GL_{2n}
,
H=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n}
, and
X=H\backslash G
. The hyperspherical dual
\check{M}=(T^{*}(\mathrm{Std}_{2n})\oplus(\mathfrak{gl}_{2n}/\mathfrak{sp}_{2n})^{*})\times^{\Sp_{2n}}\GL_{2n}
. One can take
V=\Std_{2n}\in\Rep(\GL_{2n})
and
W=V^{*}
. In this case, there will be multiple choices of
\lambda\in X_{*}(T_{H})
such that
\lambda^{+}=\lambda_{V}
: One can take
\lambda=\lambda_{1}:=((1,0\dots,0),(0,\dots,0))
or
\lambda=\lambda_{2}:=((0,\dots,0),(1,0,\dots,0))
, and these are all possible choices of
\lambda
up to
W_{H}
permutation. In this case,
?THM?
predicts that
?THM?
is satisfied for
(\lambda,\mu)=(\lambda_{1},-\lambda_{1})
and
(\lambda,\mu)=(\lambda_{2},-\lambda_{2})
.

5.3.
Determine the constant

This section will only be used in
§7.1
and can be safely skipped on a first reading.

In this section, we develop an effective way to determine the number
\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}
in (
5.22
) under
?THM?
and
?THM?
. This method will be applied later in the Rankin–Selberg case
§7.1.2
. The method also works for
?THM?
,
?THM?
,
?THM?
, and many other examples. We leave the details to the reader.

Remark 5.13
.

We are not developing a method to verify
?THM?
. We do not know an effective way to verify the existence of
b
in
loc. cit
when it indeed exists. Instead, we are assuming the existence of
b
, and try to determine the relation between
b
and
b_{\mathrm{geo}}
defined via (
5.18
) and (
5.19
). However, when the Soergel functor (
5.26
) is fully faithful on the objects we care about,
11
11

11

This is highly possible when
X
is homogeneous and strongly tempered. The full faithfulness has been proved for
?THM?
,
?THM?
in
[
7
]
,
[
8
]
.
our method can be used to prove the existence of
b
.

5.3.1.
Setup

We refer to
§2.5
for notations on Lie theory.

We first work under the general setup
?THM?
.

Define

(5.24)

R_{G}:=\Gamma(B(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D)),\underline{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma(BG\times B\mathbb{G}_{m},\underline{k})

and

(5.25)

\overline{R}_{G}:=\Gamma(BL^{+}G,\underline{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma(BG,\underline{k}).

We have
R_{G}=\overline{R}_{G}[\hbar]
. Moreover, it is well-known that
R_{G}=R_{T}^{W_{G}}
and
R_{T}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})[\hbar]
.

Consider the functor taking global sections

(5.26)

\Gamma:\Sat_{X,\hbar}=\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(LX/L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))\to\mathrm{Mod}(R_{G})

and

(5.27)

\Gamma:\Sat_{G,\hbar}=\mathrm{Shv}_{*}(\hk^{\mathrm{l}}_{G})\to\mathrm{Mod}(R_{G}\otimes_{k[\hbar]}R_{G}).

We have the formula

(5.28)

\Gamma(\mathcal{K}*\mathcal{F})=\Gamma(\mathcal{K})\otimes_{R_{G}}\Gamma(\mathcal{F})

for nice
12
12

12

For
\mathcal{K}
belonging to the full-subcategory of
\Sat_{X,\hbar}
generated by
\Sat_{G,\hbar}
and
\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}
, the argument in
[
6
, Corollary B.4.2]
works. This is enough for our purpose.
objects
\mathcal{K}\in\Sat_{G,\hbar}
and
\mathcal{F}\in\Sat_{X,\hbar}
.

For each
V\in\Rep(\check{G})^{\heartsuit,\omega}
, define

(5.29)

M_{V}:=\Gamma(\IC_{V})\in\mathrm{Mod}(R_{G}\otimes_{k[\hbar]}R_{G})

and

(5.30)

\overline{M}_{V}:=M\otimes_{k[\hbar]}k.

Now assume
?THM?
and use the notations there. We know

(5.31)

R_{H}=\Gamma(\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}})\in\mathrm{Mod}(R_{G})

where the
R_{G}
-module structure on
R_{H}
is given by the ring map
R_{G}\to R_{H}
induced by the natural map
BH\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\to BG\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
which is inclusion on the first actor and identity on the second factor.

Given each local special cohomological correspondence

(5.32)

c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\Hom^{0}(\IC_{V}*\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle d_{V}\rangle),

we get a map

(5.33)

c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}:=\Gamma(c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}})\in\Hom^{0}(M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}R_{H},R_{H}\langle d_{V}\rangle)

Therefore, by the construction of
c_{V\otimes W}^{\mathrm{l}}
in
?THM?
(
2
)
, we get

(5.34)

c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}-c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}=\hbar\cdot c_{V\otimes W}^{\mathrm{coh}}\in\Hom^{0}(M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\otimes_{R_{G}}R_{H},R_{H}\langle d_{V}+d_{W}\rangle).

where
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}:M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M_{W}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{V}
is induced from the unique element in

\Hom^{0}_{\Sat_{G,\hbar}}(\IC_{V}*\IC_{W},\IC_{W}*\IC_{V})

lifting the natural commutativity constraint in
\Sat_{G}
obtained from the fusion procedure.
13
13

13

Here, when writing a map whose source is a tensor product and the map is a priori only defined on certain tensor factors, we always mean extending the map trivially to other tensor factors. We will use this convention throughout this section without mention to simplify notations.

From now on, we work under
?THM?
and
?THM?
, and we take
c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}=c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}},c_{W}^{\mathrm{l}}=c_{W,\mu}^{\mathrm{l}}
constructed in (
5.17
). In this case, we have a natural map
b^{\mathrm{coh}}_{\mathrm{geo}}:M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\to M_{\mathrm{triv}}=R_{G}
by applying
\Gamma
to
b_{\mathrm{geo}}
in (
5.19
). Then the map
c_{V\otimes W}^{\mathrm{coh}}
can be identified as

(5.35)

c_{V\otimes W}^{\mathrm{coh}}=\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}\cdot b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}:M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\otimes_{R_{G}}R_{H}\to R_{H}

The relation (
5.34
) becomes

(5.36)

c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ c_{W,\mu}^{\mathrm{coh}}-c_{W,\mu}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}=\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}\hbar\cdot b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}

Therefore, to determine the scalar
\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}
, we only need to write down the maps
c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{coh}}
,
c_{W,\mu}^{\mathrm{coh}}
,
b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}
, and
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}
. The determination of these maps will be the subject of the rest of this section.

5.3.2.
Description of
M_{V}

We first describe
M_{V}\in\mathrm{Mod}(R_{G}\otimes_{k[\hbar]}R_{G})
defined in (
5.29
).

Recall that the parabolic subgroup
P_{G,\lambda}\subset G
in (
5.20
). Similarly, we have the parabolic subgroup
P_{H,\lambda}\subset H
.

We define
W_{G,\lambda}:=W_{P_{G,\lambda}}\subset W_{G}
and
W_{H,\lambda}:=W_{P_{H,\lambda}}\subset W_{H}
which are the Weyl groups of the Levi groups of the corresponding parabolic subgroups.

In this case, we have

(5.37)

M_{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma(B(\mathcal{P}_{G,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D)),\underline{k})\langle d_{G,\lambda}\rangle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar]\langle d_{G,\lambda}\rangle\in\mathrm{Mod}(R_{G}\otimes_{k[\hbar]}R_{G}).

The
R_{G}
-bimodule structure can be seen as follows: The right
R_{G}
-module structure on
M_{V}
is induced by the natural inclusion map
\overrightarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l},V}:B(\mathcal{P}_{G,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D))\to B(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))
which is the natural inclusion

(5.38)

i_{G,\lambda}:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G}}[\hbar]\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar].

The left
R_{G}
-module structure on
M_{V}
is induced by the map
\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l},V}:B(\mathcal{P}_{G,\lambda}\rtimes\Aut(D))\to B(L^{+}G\rtimes\Aut(D))
which is identified with the map
14
14

14

This is because on homotopy types
\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{l},V}
can be identified with
B(P_{G,\lambda}\times\mathbb{G}_{m})\to B(G\times\mathbb{G}_{m})
such that
(p,a)\mapsto(p\lambda(a^{-1}),a)
, which is an easy consequence of (
5.14
).

(5.39)

\widetilde{i}_{G,\lambda}:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G}}[\hbar]\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar]

induced from the pull-back along
\mathfrak{t}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathfrak{t}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
given by
(X,a)\mapsto(X-a\cdot d\lambda,a)
.

5.3.3.
Description of
c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{coh}}

Now we describe the map
c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{coh}}:M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}R_{H}\to R_{H}
.

Let
r:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})[\hbar]\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})[\hbar]
be the map induced by the natural inclusion. Then it is easy to see from definition of
c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}
in (
5.17
) that
c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{coh}}
can be identified with

(5.40)

\begin{split}&\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar]\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G}}[\hbar]}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}[\hbar]\langle d_{G,\lambda}\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{r\otimes i_{H,\lambda}}&\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}[\hbar]\langle d_{G,\lambda}\rangle\\
\xrightarrow{\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l},!}}&\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}[\hbar]\langle d_{G,\lambda}-2d_{H,\lambda}+2\delta_{\lambda,Y}\rangle\end{split}

where the map
\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}
is the top arrow in (
5.15
), and by
\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l},!}
we mean integration along the derived fundamental class
[\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l}}]
. The map
r:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar]\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}[\hbar]
is induced from the natural inclusion
\mathfrak{t}_{H}\to\mathfrak{t}
, and the map
i_{H,\lambda}:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}[\hbar]\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}[\hbar]
is defined in the same way as
i_{G,\lambda}
.

Furthermore, consider
\ch(D_{\lambda,Y})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}\cdot X^{*}(T_{H}\times\mathbb{G}_{m})
which is the character of the defect module
D_{\lambda,Y}
defined in
?THM?
. We can take its top Chern class

(5.41)

c_{\mathrm{top}}(\ch(D_{\lambda,Y}))\in\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})[\hbar]

Let us also consider the map

(5.42)

p_{H,\lambda}:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}\langle-2d_{H,\lambda}\rangle

given by integration along
BP_{H,\lambda}\to BH
. It is easy to see that the map

(5.43)

\overleftarrow{h}^{+,Y}_{\mathrm{l},!}:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}[\hbar]\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}\langle-2d_{H,\lambda}+2\delta_{\lambda,Y}\rangle.

can be identified with the composition

(5.44)

\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}[\hbar]\xrightarrow{\cdot c_{\mathrm{top}}(\ch(D_{\lambda,Y}))}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}[\hbar]\langle 2\delta_{\lambda,Y}\rangle\xrightarrow{p_{H,\lambda}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}[\hbar]\langle-2d_{H,\lambda}+2\delta_{\lambda,Y}\rangle.

Remark 5.14
.

The map
p_{H,\lambda}
is the unique graded
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}
-linear map sending
c_{\mathrm{top}}(\ch(\mathfrak{h}/\Lie(P_{H,\lambda})))\in\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}\langle 2d_{H,\lambda}\rangle
to
|W_{H}/W_{H,\lambda}|\in\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}
.
15
15

15

Since we are regarding
\mathfrak{g}
as a
right

G
-module, our
\ch(\mathfrak{h}/\Lie(P_{H,\lambda}))
is different from the usual convention by a minus sign.
Here, the
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}
-linear structure on
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H,\lambda}}
is given by
\widetilde{i}_{H,\lambda}
, which is defined in the same way as
\widetilde{i}_{G,\lambda}
.

5.3.4.
Description of
b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}

Now we describe the map
b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}:M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\to R_{G}
.

Choose
w\in W_{G}
such that
w(\mu)=-\lambda
. Consider the map
a_{G,\lambda,\mu}:\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar]\to\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\mu}}[\hbar]
induced by pull-back along the map
\mathfrak{t}\times\mathbb{A}\to\mathfrak{t}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
given by
(X,a)\mapsto(wX-a\cdot d\lambda,a)
. Then the map
b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}
can be identifies with

(5.45)

\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar]\langle d_{G,\lambda}\rangle\otimes_{\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G}}[\hbar]}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\mu}}[\hbar]\langle d_{G,\lambda}\rangle\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes a_{G,\lambda,\mu}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G,\lambda}}[\hbar]\langle 2d_{G,\lambda}\rangle\xrightarrow{p_{G,\lambda}}\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G}}[\hbar]

where the map
p_{G,\lambda}
is defined similarly as (
5.42
).

5.3.5.
Description of
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}

Now we describe the map
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}:M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M_{W}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{V}
.

By
[
5
, §6.2, Lemma 13]
, we know that
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}
is the unique graded map lifting
\overline{M}_{V}\otimes_{\overline{R}_{G}}\overline{M}_{W}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\overline{M}_{W}\otimes_{\overline{R}_{G}}\overline{M}_{V}
given by the natural commutativity constraint in
\mathrm{Mod}(\overline{R}_{G})
(i.e.
x\otimes y\mapsto(-1)^{|x||y|}y\otimes x
)

Remark 5.15
.

Although the above gives a characterization of the map
\mathrm{sw}_{V,V^{*}}
, we do not know a general way to write it down for all minuscule
V
.

6.
Higher period integrals and derivatives of
L
-functions

In this section, we combine the results in previous sections to get a higher period integral formula (
?THM?
).

•

In
§6.1
, we recall the geometric isotypic part of the
X
-period, which has been considered in many previous works.

•

In
§6.2
, we discuss the Hecke action on the geometric isotypic part.

•

In
§6.3
, we restrict to the Poisson-pure case and get the main result
?THM?
.

6.1.
Geometric isotypic part of period integral

For any affine smooth
G
-variety
X
, we have the period sheaf

\mathcal{P}_{X}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})

constructed in
§4.3.4
. We would like to construct some “higher period integrals”, which can be related to higher derivatives of
L
-functions after restricting the “integrals” to the “
\pi
-isotypic part” for every (everywhere unramified) cuspidal automorphic representation
\pi\in\Irr(G(\mathbb{A}_{F}))
. For this purpose, we need to make sense of the “isotypic part of the period” and reasonably define higher integrals.

6.1.1.
Geometric period integral

We now come to the definition of geometric period integral, which is a geometric enhancement of the period integral.

Definition 6.1
.

Consider
\pi:\Bun_{G}^{X}\to\Bun_{G}
. We define the
geometric
X
-period integral
to be the functor
\int_{X}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect}
such that
\int_{X}=\Gamma_{c}\circ\pi^{*}
.

Remark 6.2
.

Here we are using
!
-push for morphisms locally of finite type between Artin stacks locally of finite type. We did not include this in our
\mathrm{Shv}
due to laziness in writing the article. However, by
?THM?
, our sheaf theory is the same as the usual sheaf theory on Artin stacks locally of finite type. Therefore, we can use the six-functor formalism developed in
[
18
]
.

Remark 6.3
.

The functor
\int_{X}
can be understood using the period sheaf as follows: Consider the functor
\Gamma_{c}\circ\Delta_{\Bun_{G}}^{*}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G}\times\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect}
, where
\Delta_{\Bun_{G}}:\Bun_{G}\to\Bun_{G}\times\Bun_{G}
is the diagonal map. By base change, we have
\int_{X}=\Gamma_{c}\circ\Delta_{\Bun_{G}}^{*}(\mathcal{P}_{X}\boxtimes-)
. By
[
2
, Corollary 3.3.7]
, we know that
\Gamma_{c}\circ\Delta_{\Bun_{G}}^{*}|_{\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})^{\otimes 2}}=\ev_{\mathrm{Mir}}
is the counit map for the Miraculous duality on
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
. By
[
2
, Proposition 3.4.6]
, we know
\int_{X}|_{\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})}=\ev_{\mathrm{Mir}}(\mathcal{P}_{X}^{r}\otimes-)
, where
\mathcal{P}_{X}^{r}=\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}*\mathcal{P}_{X}
is the image of
\mathcal{P}_{X}
under the right adjoint (to the forgetful functor)
\mathrm{P}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
which is given by the Beilinson’s spectral projector. Note that it is conjectured in
[
4
, §12.4]
that the period sheaf after spectral projection
\mathcal{P}_{X}^{r}
should correspond to the
L
-sheaf defined in
loc.cit.
. This gives a way to understand
\int_{X}|_{\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})}
in terms of miraculous duality and the global conjecture made in
[
4
]
.

Remark 6.4
.

Note that the functor
\int_{X}|_{\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})}:\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Vect}
preserves compact objects. This observation will be used in
[
32
]
to relate fake special cycle classes and isotypic part of special cycle classes on Shtukas via the functoriality of categorical trace.

6.1.2.
Hecke eigensheaves

We propose a definition of higher integrals using the period sheaf and the Hecke eigensheaves. For this purpose, we recall the notion of Hecke eigensheaf following
[
3
, §13]
:

Definition 6.5
.

For
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}}(k)
(i.e. a map
i_{\sigma}:\pt\to\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}}
), a Hecke eigensheaf for
\sigma
is an object in

\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma}:=\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes_{\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}})}\mathrm{Vect}

where
\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}})
acts on
\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})
via the spectral action
[
3
, Theorem 0.7.4]
, and the action of
\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}})
on
\mathrm{Vect}
is given by
i_{\sigma}^{*}:\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}})\to\mathrm{Vect}
.

We summarize some useful properties relating to Hecke eigensheaves:

Proposition 6.6
.

The following holds:

(1)

The map

\mathrm{id}\otimes i_{\sigma,*}:\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma}=\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\otimes_{\mathrm{QCoh}(\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}})}\mathrm{Vect}\to\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})

admits left adjoint
\mathrm{id}\otimes i_{\sigma}^{*}
;

(2)

[
3
, §13.1.10]
The map

\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\otimes i_{\sigma,*}}\mathrm{Shv}_{\mathrm{Nilp}}(\Bun_{G})\subset\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})

admits left adjoint
\mathrm{P}_{\sigma}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma}
.

Given
?THM?
(
2
)
, we make the following definition:

Definition 6.7
.

For
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{res}}(k)
, we define the
Whittaker normalized
Hecke eigensheaf of
\sigma
to be

\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{Whit}}:=\mathrm{P}_{\sigma}(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Whit}}^{\mathrm{norm}})\in\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma},

where
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Whit}}^{\mathrm{norm}}
is the normalized Whittaker period sheaf defined in
§2.9
.

In concrete terms, giving a Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
is equivalent to giving the object
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
together with compatible isomorphisms

\IC_{c^{*}V}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\square$}\raisebox{0.49515pt}{\hskip 0.89996pt\Large$\star$}\ (\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\boxtimes\underline{k}_{C})\cong\overrightarrow{h}_{\{1\},\mathrm{g},!}(\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{g}}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\otimes\IC_{V})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}V_{\sigma}\boxtimes\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Shv}(C\times\Bun_{G})

for
V\in\Rep(\check{G})
. Here the two maps
\overleftarrow{h}_{\mathrm{g}},\overrightarrow{h}_{\{1\},\mathrm{g}}
are defined in
§4.3.1
, and we recall that they form the diagram

The sheaf
V_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Lisse}(C)
is the value of
\sigma:\Rep(\check{G})\to\mathrm{QLisse}(C)
at
V\in\Rep(\check{G})
. Here,
\mathrm{Lisse}(C)
(resp.
\mathrm{QLisse}(C)
) is the category of lisse sheaves (resp. left completion of ind-lisse sheaves) on
C
defined in
[
3
, §1.2]
.
In particular, we know that for each
V\in\Rep(\check{G})

(6.1)

\IC_{c^{*}V}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\square$}\raisebox{0.49515pt}{\hskip 0.89996pt\Large$\star$}\ \mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}

where the action
\square
\star
is the action of
\Sat_{G,\hbar}
on
\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
defined in
§4.3.2
.

6.1.3.
Geometric isotypic part

Definition 6.8
.

We define
geometric
\sigma
-isotypic
X
-period integral
to be the vector space

\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Vect}.

6.2.
Hecke actions and cocommutator relations

In this section, we study the Hecke action on
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
coming from special cohomological correspondences on
\mathcal{P}_{X}
. In the Language of
[
4
]
, these Hecke actions come from the action of the
RTF-algebra
on the period sheaf. For a more detailed discussion on this perspective, we refer to
[
4
, §16.3]
.

6.2.1.
Hecke actions

Suppose we are given a cohomological correspondence

c_{V}^{\mathrm{g}}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathrm{g}}_{G,\{1\}},\IC_{V}}(\mathcal{P}_{X},\mathcal{P}_{X}\langle d_{V}-2\rangle)=\Hom^{0}(\IC_{V}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\square$}\raisebox{0.49515pt}{\hskip 0.89996pt\Large$\star$}\ \mathcal{P}_{X}\langle-d_{V}+2\rangle,\mathcal{P}_{X}),

we can define the Hecke action map as the following composition

(6.2)

\begin{split}a_{V,\sigma}:\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\langle-d_{V}+2\rangle\otimes\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}&=\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\langle-d_{V}+2\rangle\otimes\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{G},\mathcal{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{G},\mathcal{P}_{X}\otimes(\IC_{c^{*}V}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\square$}\raisebox{0.49515pt}{\hskip 0.89996pt\Large$\star$}\ \mathbb{L}_{\sigma}))\langle-d_{V}+2\rangle\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{G},(\IC_{V}\makebox[0.0pt][l]{$\square$}\raisebox{0.49515pt}{\hskip 0.89996pt\Large$\star$}\ \mathcal{P}_{X})\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})\langle-d_{V}+2\rangle\\
&\to\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{G},\mathcal{P}_{X}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})\\
&=\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\end{split}

Moreover, given a local special cohomological correspondence datum
(I,V^{I},\{c_{V^{x}}^{\mathrm{l}}\}_{x\in I})
for
I=[r]
as in
?THM?
, we can form special global cohomological correspondences
\{c_{V^{x}}^{\mathrm{g}}\}_{x\in I}
as in (
4.39
) and define

(6.3)

a_{V^{I},\sigma}=a_{V^{1},\sigma}\circ\cdots\circ(\underbrace{\mathrm{id}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathrm{id}}_{(r-1)\penalty\ \mathrm{times}}\otimes a_{V^{r},\sigma}):\bigotimes_{x\in I}\Gamma(C,V^{x}_{\sigma})\langle-d_{I}+2r\rangle\otimes\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\to\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}.

6.2.2.
Commutator relations

Now consider the special cohomological correspondences
c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}},c_{W}^{\mathrm{l}},c_{V\otimes W}^{\mathrm{l}}
in
?THM?
. Note that we have a natural map induced by the cup product

(6.4)

\begin{split}\cup:\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\otimes\Gamma(C,W_{\sigma})\to\Gamma(C,(V\otimes W)_{\sigma})\end{split}.

We have the following corollary of
?THM?
:

Corollary 6.9
.

Under
?THM?
, assume
?THM?
is true. We have the following identity of maps

a_{V\boxtimes W,\sigma}-a_{W\boxtimes V,\sigma}=a_{V\otimes W,\sigma}\circ(\cup\otimes\mathrm{id}):\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\otimes\Gamma(C,W_{\sigma})\langle-d_{V}-d_{W}+4\rangle\otimes\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\to\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}.

The proof is straightforward, and we omit it.

6.3.
Middle-dimensional case

In this section, we keep working under
?THM?
and assuming further
?THM?
, which gives us a
G
-invariant bilinear form
b:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}
. We assume this bilinear form is non-degenerate.

6.3.1.
Clifford relations

Consider the natural map

(6.5)

\begin{split}\ev_{b,\sigma}:&\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\otimes\Gamma(C,W_{\sigma})\langle 2\rangle\\
\to&\Gamma(C,(V\otimes W)_{\sigma})\langle 2\rangle\\
\to&\Gamma(C,\underline{k})\langle 2\rangle\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}&k\end{split}.

Here, the first map is the cup product, the second map is induced by
b:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}
, the third map is the natural isomorphism given by the fundamental class of
C
.

We have the following reformulation of
?THM?
:

Corollary 6.10
.

Under
?THM?
and
?THM?
, assume
?THM?
is true. We have the following identity of maps

a_{V\boxtimes W,\sigma}-a_{W\boxtimes V,\sigma}=\ev_{b,\sigma}\otimes\mathrm{id}:\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\otimes\Gamma(C,W_{\sigma})\langle 2\rangle\otimes\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\to\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}.

6.3.2.
Clifford algebra

Continue with the setting in the previous section. We now construct an action of a Clifford algebra on the geometric isotypic part
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
.

We first construct a symplectic
\check{G}
-representation
(K,\omega_{K})
as follows: When
V=W
, it is easy to see that
b
is a symplectic form, we can take
(K,\omega_{K}):=(V,b)
; when
V
and
W
are not necessarily isomorphic, we can take
(K,\omega_{K}):=(V\oplus W,\omega_{\mathrm{can}})
where
\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
is the canonical symplectic form constructed in (
2.12
). Here we use
b
to identify
V\cong W^{*}
.

Define
M:=\Gamma(C,K_{\rho})\langle 1\rangle
. It is equipped with a symplectic pairing
\omega_{M}
coming from the symplectic pairing
\omega_{K}
and cup product. From the map (
6.2
) (take
I
=[1]), we get an action of the free tensor algebra
M^{\otimes}
on
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
denoted by

(6.6)

a_{M,\sigma}:M^{\otimes}\otimes\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\to\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}.

Suppose we are further under
?THM?
, we can also use
b_{\mathrm{geo}}
in (
5.19
) instead of
b=b_{\lambda,\mu}
in (
5.21
). The same construction as above gives us a symplectic form
\omega_{K,\mathrm{geo}}
on
K
and a symplectic form
\omega_{M,\mathrm{geo}}
on
M
. We have
\omega_{M}=\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}\cdot\omega_{M,\mathrm{geo}}
.
We make the following assumption for simplicity:

Assumption 6.11
.

M\in\mathrm{Vect}^{\heartsuit}
.

Then we get the following reformulation of
?THM?
:

Corollary 6.12
.

Suppose we are in
?THM?
and further assume
?THM?
,
?THM?
, and
?THM?
. Suppose
?THM?
is true, then the action (
6.6
) factors through the Clifford algebra
\operatorname{Cl}(M)=\operatorname{Cl}(M,\omega_{M})
introduced in
§3.1.1
, hence gives us an action map

(6.7)

a_{M,\sigma}:\operatorname{Cl}(M)\otimes\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\to\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}.

Remark 6.13
.

?THM?
made in
?THM?
can be weakened to only require that
\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})
and
\Gamma(C,W_{\sigma})
are concentrated in a single degree respectively without difficulty.

6.3.3.
Producing Kolyvagin system

Now we would like to apply the machinery in
§3
. We make the following assumption:

Assumption 6.14
.

The geometric
\sigma
-isotypic
X
-period
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Vect}
is a perfect complex.

We need Frobenius automorphisms on
M
and
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
. We make the following setup:

Setting 6.15
.

We choose
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(k)
(i.e. a Weil
\check{G}
-local system on
C
) and a Weil Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}\in\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma}^{\Frob^{*}}
, where
\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma}
is the category of Hecke eigensheaves defined in
?THM?
.

Note that for each
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\check{G}}^{\mathrm{arith}}(k)
, the Whittaker normalized Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{Whit}}
defined in
?THM?
gives a particular choice of Weil Hecke eigensheaf.

Given a Weil Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
, continuing with the assumptions in last section, we get a triple
(M,\omega,F)
as in
?THM?
where
F
is given by the Frobenius endomorphism of
M=\Gamma(C,K_{\sigma})\langle 1\rangle
, and
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
is an
F
-equivariant
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-module. We can construct the Kolyvagin system

(6.8)

\{z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

following
?THM?
. Then
?THM?
gives us the following result, which can be regarded as a higher period formula:

Theorem 6.16
.

Keep the same assumptions as
?THM?
. The following identity holds for any
r\geq 0
:

(6.9)

\omega_{r,\mathrm{geo}}(z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r},z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r})=\beta_{\lambda,\mu,X}(\log q)^{-r}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}(q^{(g-1)\dim(K)s}L(K_{\sigma},s+1/2))

where
\beta_{\lambda,\mu,X}:=\kappa_{\lambda,\mu}^{-r}\lambda(\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})(-1)^{r/2}
. The bilinear form
\omega_{r,\mathrm{geo}}:(M^{\otimes r})^{\otimes 2}\to k
is induced from
\omega_{M,\mathrm{geo}}:M^{\otimes 2}\to k
as in (
2.10
), the factor
\lambda(\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma})
is defined in (
3.24
).

Proof.

One just note that we have
\dim(M)=\dim\Gamma(C,K_{\sigma})\langle 1\rangle=(0|2(g-1)\dim(K))
,

L(K_{\sigma},s+1/2)=L(M,F,s),

and
z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma},r}
is non-zero only if
r
is even. Then the formula follows from
?THM?
.
∎

7.
Example: higher Rankin–Selberg convolutions

In this section, we consider the example
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
and
X=\GL_{n}
, where
G
acts on
X
via

x\cdot(g_{n},g_{n-1})=g_{n-1}^{-1}xg_{n}

for
(g_{n},g_{n-1})\in\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
and
x\in X=\GL_{n}
, where we regard
\GL_{n-1}
as a subgroup of
\GL_{n}
via
g_{n-1}\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(g_{n-1},1)
. In this case, we have
\check{G}=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
and
\check{M}=T^{*}(\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n-1})
, where the
\mathbb{G}_{\mathrm{gr}}
-action on
\check{M}
is the scaling action (i.e. weight one on both
\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n-1}
and
(\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n-1})^{*}
).

•

In
§7.1
, we discuss the local aspect of this example.

•

In
§7.2
, we discuss the global aspect of this example.

•

In
§7.3
, we combine the input and get the higher Rankin–Selberg integral formula
?THM?
.

7.1.
Local input

We first discuss the local aspect of this example. The key result is
?THM?
.

7.1.1.
Construction of local special cohomological correspondences

?THM?
in this case is proved in
[
7
]
. In particular, it tells us that
?THM?
holds for
V:=\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n-1}
,
W:=\mathrm{Std}_{n}^{*}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n-1}^{*}
, and we are going to introduce the local special cohomological correspondences
c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}
and
c_{W}^{\mathrm{l}}
used in
loc.cit
.
We directly apply the construction in
§5.1
. Using the notations in
loc.cit
, we have
H=\GL_{n-1}
regarded as a subgroup of
G=\GL_{n}\times\GL_{n-1}
via diagonal embedding. Under the standard identification
X_{*}(T)=\mathbb{Z}^{n}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
. We have

\lambda_{V}=((1,0,\dots,0),(1,0,\dots,0))\in X_{*}(T)

and

\lambda_{W}=((0,\dots,0,-1),(0,\dots,0,-1))\in X_{*}(T).

Note also that
X_{*}(T_{H})=\mathbb{Z}^{n-1}
. We take

\lambda=\lambda_{\mathrm{Std}_{n-1}}:=(1,0,\dots,0)\in X_{*}(T_{H})^{+},

and
\mu=-\lambda
. Then we have
\lambda^{+}=\lambda_{V}
and
\mu^{+}=\lambda_{W}
. The construction (
5.17
) gives us

(7.1)

c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}:=c_{V,\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathring{X},\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{V}}(\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle 1\rangle),c_{W}^{\mathrm{l}}:=c_{W,-\lambda}^{\mathrm{l}}\in\mathrm{Corr}_{\hk^{\mathring{X},\mathrm{l}}_{G},\IC_{W}}(\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle 1\rangle).

7.1.2.
Determine the number
\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}
in (
5.22
)

Now we can apply the method in
§5.3
to determine the number
\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}
in (
5.22
). The result is the following:

Proposition 7.1
.

We have
\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}=(-1)^{n-1}
. In other word, we have

c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}\circ c_{W}^{\mathrm{l}}-c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}\circ c_{W}^{\mathrm{l}}=(-1)^{n-1}\hbar\cdot b_{\mathrm{geo}}\in\Hom^{0}(\IC_{V\otimes W}*\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}},\delta^{X}_{\mathrm{l}}\langle 2\rangle).

Here
b_{\mathrm{geo}}:V\otimes W\to\mathrm{triv}
is defined in (
5.19
).

Remark 7.2
.

The number
\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}
can also be read off from the proof of
?THM?
in this case given in
[
7
]
. Since we are using different conventions on Hecke actions, to avoid confusion, we prefer to provide a direct approach to determine this number.

Proof of
?THM?
.

Using the notation in
§5.3
, we have
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})=k[x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{n-1}]
,
\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})=k[z_{1},\dots,z_{n-1}]
, such that the restriction along
\mathfrak{t}_{H}\to\mathfrak{t}
is given by
x_{i}\mapsto z_{i}
,
y_{i}\mapsto z_{i}
.

Let

(7.2)

c_{i}:=\sum_{J\subset[n],|J|=i}\prod_{j\in J}x_{j}

(7.3)

d_{i}:=\sum_{J\subset[n-1],|J|=i}\prod_{j\in J}y_{j}

(7.4)

e_{i}:=\sum_{J\subset[n-1],|J|=i}\prod_{j\in J}z_{j}

(7.5)

x:=x_{1}

(7.6)

y:=y_{1}

(7.7)

\overline{c}_{i}:=\sum_{J\subset[n]-\{1\},|J|=i}\prod_{j\in J}x_{j}

(7.8)

\overline{d}_{i}:=\sum_{J\subset[n-1]-\{1\},|J|=i}\prod_{j\in J}y_{j}

(7.9)

\overline{e}_{i}:=\sum_{J\subset[n-1]-\{1\},|J|=i}\prod_{j\in J}z_{j}

We have

(7.10)

R_{G}=\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t})^{W_{G}}[\hbar]=k[c_{1},\dots,c_{n},d_{1},\dots,d_{n-1},\hbar]

(7.11)

R_{H}=\mathcal{O}(\mathfrak{t}_{H})^{W_{H}}[\hbar]=k[e_{1},\cdots,e_{n-1},\hbar]

and

(7.12)

M_{V}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}M_{W}=k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\langle 2n-3\rangle.

We first describe the
(R_{G},R_{G})
-bimodule structure on
M_{V}
following
§5.3.2
. The right
R_{G}
-module structure on
M_{V}
is given by the ring map

(7.13)

\begin{split}i_{G,\lambda}:k[c_{1},\dots,c_{n},d_{1},\cdots,d_{n-1},\hbar]&\to k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\\
c_{i}&\mapsto x\overline{c}_{i-1}+\overline{c}_{i}\\
d_{i}&\mapsto y\overline{d}_{i-1}+\overline{d}_{i}\\
\hbar&\mapsto\hbar\end{split}.

The left
R_{G}
-module structure on
M_{V}
is given by the ring map

(7.14)

\begin{split}\widetilde{i}_{G,\lambda}:k[c_{1},\dots,c_{n},d_{1},\dots,d_{n-1},\hbar]&\to k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\\
c_{i}&\mapsto(x-\hbar)\overline{c}_{i-1}+\overline{c}_{i}\\
d_{i}&\mapsto(y-\hbar)\overline{d}_{i-1}+\overline{d}_{i}\\
\hbar&\mapsto\hbar\end{split}.

We can similarly describe the
(R_{G},R_{G})
-bimodule structure on
M_{W}
: the right
R_{G}
-module structure on
M_{W}
in still given by the same formula (
7.13
), while the left
R_{G}
-module structure on
M_{W}
is given by the ring map

(7.15)

\begin{split}\widetilde{i}_{G,-\lambda}:k[c_{1},\dots,c_{n},d_{1},\dots,d_{n-1},\hbar]&\to k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\\
c_{i}&\mapsto(x+\hbar)\overline{c}_{i-1}+\overline{c}_{i}\\
d_{i}&\mapsto(y+\hbar)\overline{d}_{i-1}+\overline{d}_{i}\\
\hbar&\mapsto\hbar\end{split}.

Following
§5.3.3
, the map
c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}:M_{V}\langle-1\rangle\otimes_{R_{G}}R_{H}\to R_{H}
can be identified with the composition

(7.16)

\begin{split}c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}:&k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\otimes_{R_{G}}k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},\hbar]\langle 2n-4\rangle\\
\to&k[z,\overline{e}_{1},\dots,\overline{e}_{n-2},\hbar]\langle 2n-4\rangle\\
\to&k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-2},\hbar]\end{split}

The first map in (
7.16
) is
k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]
-linear on the left and
k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},\hbar]
-linear on the right (both consider the most natural module structure). The second map in (
7.16
) is the unique
k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},\hbar]
-linear map satisfies
z^{n-2}\mapsto(-1)^{n}
(see
?THM?
). Here, the linearity is defined via
\widetilde{i}_{H,\lambda}
.

Similarly, the map
c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}:M_{W}\langle-1\rangle\otimes_{R_{G}}R_{H}\to R_{H}
can be identified with the composition

(7.17)

\begin{split}c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}:&k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\otimes_{R_{G}}k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},\hbar]\langle 2n-4\rangle\\
\to&k[z,\overline{e}_{1},\dots,\overline{e}_{n-2},\hbar]\langle 2n-4\rangle\\
\to&k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-2},\hbar]\end{split}

The first map in (
7.17
) is
k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]
-linear on the left and
k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},\hbar]
-linear on the right (both consider the most natural module structure). The second map in (
7.17
) is the unique
k[e_{1},\dots,e_{n-1},\hbar]
-linear map satisfies
z^{n-2}\mapsto 1
. Here, the linearity is defined via
\widetilde{i}_{H,-\lambda}
.

Following
§5.3.4
, the map
b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}
can be identified with the composition

(7.18)

\begin{split}b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}:&k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\otimes_{R_{G}}k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\langle 4n-8\rangle\\
\to&k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]\langle 4n-8\rangle\\
\to&k[c_{1},\dots,c_{n},d_{1},\dots,d_{n-1},\hbar]\end{split}

The first map in (
7.18
) is the unique
k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]
-bilinear map where the left module structure on the second line comes from the twisted ring map
x\mapsto x-\hbar
,
y\mapsto y-\hbar
(and no twist on other generators). The second map in (
7.18
) is the unique
k[x,\overline{c}_{1},\dots,\overline{c}_{n-1},y,\overline{d}_{1},\dots,\overline{d}_{n-2},\hbar]
-linear map such that
x^{n-1}y^{n-2}\mapsto-1
. Here, the linearity is defined via
\widetilde{i}_{G,\lambda}
.

The only thing needs extra work is the description of
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}
, which is given by the following lemma:

Lemma 7.3
.

The map
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}:M_{V}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\to M_{W}\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{V}
satisfies

\begin{split}x^{i}y^{s}\otimes x^{j}y^{t}\mapsto x^{j}y^{t}\otimes x^{i}y^{s}\end{split}

for
0\leq i,j\leq n-1
,
0\leq s,t\leq n-2
such that
0\leq i+j\leq n-1
,
0\leq s+t\leq n-2
.

Proof.

This follows from the characterization of
\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}
given in
§5.3.5
via elementary arguments. We leave it to the reader.
∎

Now we can easily determine the scalar
\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}
. Consider
x^{n-1}\otimes y^{n-2}\otimes 1\in M_{V}\langle-1\rangle\otimes_{R_{G}}M_{W}\langle-1\rangle\otimes_{R_{G}}R_{H}
. We have

(7.19)

\begin{split}c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}(x^{n-1}\otimes y^{n-2}\otimes 1)&=c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}(x^{n-1}\otimes 1)\\
&=(-1)^{n}(e_{1}+(n-1)\hbar)\end{split}

(7.20)

\begin{split}c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ\mathrm{sw}_{V,W}(x^{n-1}\otimes y^{n-2}\otimes 1)&=c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}\circ c_{V}^{\mathrm{coh}}(y^{n-2}\otimes x^{n-1}\otimes 1)\\
&=c_{W}^{\mathrm{coh}}((-1)^{n}y^{n-2}\otimes(e_{1}+(n-1)\hbar))\\
&=(-1)^{n}(e_{1}+(n-2)\hbar)\end{split}

(7.21)

\begin{split}\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}\hbar\cdot b_{\mathrm{geo}}^{\mathrm{coh}}(x^{n-1}\otimes y^{n-2}\otimes 1)=-\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}\hbar\end{split}

Comparing (
7.19
) (
7.20
) (
7.21
) with (
5.36
), we get
\kappa_{\lambda,-\lambda}=(-1)^{n-1}
. This concludes the proof.

∎

7.2.
Global input

Now we turn to the global aspect of the example. The main result in this section is
?THM?
.

We write
\Bun_{n}:=\Bun_{\GL_{n}}
, and we have a connected components decomposition
\Bun_{n}=\coprod_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\Bun_{n}^{d}
according to the degree. Then we have
\Bun_{G}^{X}=\Bun_{n-1}
and the period sheaf
\mathcal{P}_{X}=\pi_{!}\underline{k}_{\Bun_{n-1}}
where

(7.22)

\pi:\Bun_{n-1}\to\Bun_{n-1}\times\Bun_{n}

is given by

\pi(\mathcal{E})=(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O})

for
\mathcal{E}\in\Bun_{n-1}
, where
\mathcal{O}
is the structure sheaf on the curve
C
.

7.2.1.
Hecke eigensheaves for
\GL_{n}

In this section, we briefly recall some properties of the (Weil) Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
for each
\sigma\in\mathrm{Loc}_{\GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{res},\mathrm{irr}}(k)
(i.e.
\sigma
is a
geometrically irreducible
rank
n
local system on
C
) constructed in
[
13
]
. Our normalization will be different from the normalization in
loc.cit
. Since the numerical statement
?THM?
is very sensitive to the Weil structure on the Hecke eigensheaf, we add a superscript
\mathrm{FGV}
in the notation
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
to distinguish it from an arbitrary Hecke eigensheaf.

Fix a line bundle
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic
. Define
\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{L}}
to be the moduli space whose
S
points is given by

(7.23)

\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{L}}(S)=\{\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\otimes\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{E}|\mathcal{E}\in\Bun_{n}(S)\}.

where we always use
\subset
to denote inclusion of
coherent sheaves
.
Denote the moduli space of rank 0 coherent sheaves on
C
by
\Coh_{0}
. Consider the moduli space
\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)\otimes\mathcal{L}}
defined by

(7.24)

\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)\otimes\mathcal{L}}(S)=\{\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\mathcal{E}_{2}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}|\textup{$\mathcal{E}_{i}\in\Bun_{i}(S)$, $\mathcal{E}_{i}/\mathcal{E}_{i-1}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Omega^{(n-1)/2-i+1}\otimes\mathcal{L}$, $i\in[n]$}\}.

Consider the moduli space

(7.25)

\mathrm{Q}_{n}^{\mathcal{L}}:=\{\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1}|(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n})\in\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)\otimes\mathcal{L}}(S),\mathcal{E}_{n+1}\in\Bun_{n}(S),\mathcal{E}_{n+1}/\mathcal{E}_{n}\in\Coh_{0}(S)\}

We have similar degree decomposition
\Coh_{0}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\Coh_{0}^{d}
and
\mathrm{Q}_{n}^{\mathcal{L}}=\coprod_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{Q}_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}
, where
\mathrm{Q}_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}
is defined by
\deg(\mathcal{E}_{n+1})=d
.

There is a natural decomposition
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV},d}
such that
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV},d}
is supported on
\Bun_{n}^{d}
.

Recall the Laumon sheaf
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}^{d}\in\mathrm{Shv}(\Coh_{0})
whose construction is given in
[
13
, §2.1]
(our
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}^{d}
is the same as
\mathcal{L}_{E}^{d}
for
E=\sigma
in
loc. cit.
). The sheaf
\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}^{d}
is a shift of an irreducible perverse sheaf and lies in (naive) cohomological degree 0 on the semisimple locus of
\Coh_{0}
.

For each
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, consider diagram

(7.26)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Q}^{\mathcal{L},d}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{L},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\Coh_{0}^{d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}\times\alpha_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}}

where the maps in (
7.26
) are defined by

v_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1})=(\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\otimes\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1})\in\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{L},d}

\ev_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1})=q(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{1}

\alpha_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1})=\mathcal{E}_{n+1}/\mathcal{E}_{n}\in\Coh_{0}^{d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}

for
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1})\in\mathrm{Q}_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}
where
q:\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the map in (
2.19
), and

\rho_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}(\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\otimes\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}

for
(\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\otimes\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{E})\in\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{L},d}
.

Define

(7.27)

\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}^{\mathcal{L},d}:=\ev_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d,*}\mathrm{AS}\otimes\alpha_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d,*}\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\langle\dim(\mathrm{Q}^{\mathcal{L},d}_{n})\rangle

where
\mathrm{AS}
is the Artin-Schreier sheaf in (
2.18
) and

(7.28)

\dim(\mathrm{Q}^{\mathcal{L},d}_{n})=\dim(\Bun_{N,\check{\rho}(\Omega)})+(d-n\deg(\mathcal{L}))n=(1-g)(\sum_{i\in[n-1]}i^{2})+(d-n\deg(\mathcal{L}))n

for
d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})\geq 0
.

The main input is the following:

Proposition 7.4
.

[
13
, §7.9,Corollary 9.3,§8.7]

(1)

There exists a unique (perverse) Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\in\mathrm{Hecke}_{\sigma}
satisfying

\rho_{n}^{\mathcal{O},d,*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}v_{n,!}^{\mathcal{O},d}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}^{\mathcal{O},d}\langle-d+n^{2}(g-1)\rangle,

where
d-n^{2}(g-1)=\dim(\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{O},d})-\dim(\Bun_{n})
(
\dim
is understood as expected dimension).

(2)

For every
d\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV},d}:=\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}|_{\Bun_{n}^{d}}
is a clean extension from a quasi-compact open substack of
\Bun_{n}^{d}
.

(3)

We have

\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{Verdier}}(\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma^{*}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}

where
\mathbb{D}^{\mathrm{Verdier}}:\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})\to\mathrm{Shv}(\Bun_{G})
is the Verdier duality functor.

Remark 7.5
.

When
n=1
, one can easily check
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
where
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}
is defined in
§2.10
.

We have the following corollary of
?THM?
:

Corollary 7.6
.

For any
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(\mathbb{F}_{q})
, we have

\rho_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d,*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}v_{n,!}^{\mathcal{L},d}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}^{\mathcal{L},d}\langle-(d-n\deg(\mathcal{L}))+n^{2}(g-1)\rangle\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma})_{\mathcal{L}}\langle-(g-1)\rangle

where
(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma})_{\mathcal{L}}
is the stalk at
\mathcal{L}\in\Pic(\mathbb{F}_{q})
of the Hecke eigensheaf
\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma}
with eigenvalue
\det\sigma
introduced in
§2.10
.

Proof of
?THM?
.

Consider
m_{\mathcal{L}}:\Bun_{n}\to\Bun_{n}
defined by

(7.29)

m_{\mathcal{L}}(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{L}\in\Bun_{n}

for
\mathcal{E}\in\Bun_{n}
. From the Hecke-eigen property of
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
, we know

(7.30)

m_{\mathcal{L}}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma})_{\mathcal{L}}\langle-(g-1)\rangle.

Consider the diagram

(7.31)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Q}^{\mathcal{L},d}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Q}^{\mathcal{O},d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{L},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun^{\prime\mathcal{O},d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n}^{d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{n}^{\mathcal{O},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}^{\mathcal{L},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m^{\prime}_{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}^{\mathcal{O},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m_{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where

(7.32)

m^{\mathrm{Q}}_{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1})=(\mathcal{E}_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1})\in\mathrm{Q}^{\mathcal{O},d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}_{n}

for
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n+1})\in\mathrm{Q}^{\mathcal{L},d}_{n}
, and

(7.33)

m^{\prime}_{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}(\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\otimes\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{E})=(\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\subset\mathcal{E}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1})\in\Bun^{\prime\mathcal{O},d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}_{n}

for
(\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\otimes\mathcal{L}\subset\mathcal{E})\in\Bun_{n}^{\prime\mathcal{L},d}
.
It is easy to see that all horizontal maps are isomorphisms. Then our desired isomorphism follows from
?THM?
(
1
)
, (
7.30
) and

m^{\mathrm{Q},*}_{\mathcal{L}^{-1}}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}^{\mathcal{O},d-n\deg(\mathcal{L})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma}^{\mathcal{L},d}.

∎

Remark 7.7
.

Our normalization is different from
[
13
]
as follows: One gets the sheaf
\Aut_{E}
for
E=\sigma
in
loc.cit
when taking
\mathcal{L}=\Omega^{(n-1)/2}
instead of
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{O}
in
?THM?
(
1
)
. Moreover,
?THM?
tells us that
\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Aut_{E}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det E})_{\Omega^{(n-1)/2}}\langle-(g-1)\rangle
for
E=\sigma
.

7.2.2.
Computing
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}

From now on, we fix
\sigma=(\sigma_{n},\sigma_{n-1})\in\mathrm{Loc}_{GL_{n}}^{\mathrm{res},\mathrm{irr}}(k)\times\mathrm{Loc}_{GL_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{res},\mathrm{irr}}(k)
. Consider the diagram

(7.34)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Q}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}_{n-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Q}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n-1}^{\prime\Omega^{-n/2},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n-1}^{d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n}^{\prime\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Bun_{n}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{\mathrm{Q}}^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{n-1}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{n-1}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v_{n}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n-1}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r^{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lrcorner}

where the right column is the top row of (
7.26
) (taking
\mathcal{L}=\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}
) and the left triangle is also the top row of (
7.26
) for
n-1
(taking
\mathcal{L}=\Omega^{-n/2}
). The map
r^{d}
is defined by

r^{d}(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{E}\oplus\mathcal{O}\in\Bun_{n}^{d}

for
\mathcal{E}\in\Bun_{n-1}^{d}
. The map
s^{d}
is defined by

s^{d}(\mathcal{E})=(\Omega^{(n-1)/2}\otimes\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}=\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{E})\in\Bun_{n}^{\prime\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}

for
\mathcal{E}\in\Bun_{n-1}^{d}
. The map
s^{d}_{\mathrm{Q}}
is defined by

s^{d}_{\mathrm{Q}}(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n-1}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n})=(\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{E}_{1}\oplus\mathcal{O}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n-1}\oplus\mathcal{O}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n}\oplus\mathcal{O})\in\mathrm{Q}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}_{n}

for
(\mathcal{E}_{1}\subset\cdots\subset\mathcal{E}_{n-1}\subset\mathcal{E}_{n})\in\mathrm{Q}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}_{n-1}
.

Note that the square in (
7.34
) is Cartesian.
Define
d_{0}:=d-(n-1)\deg(\Omega^{-n/2})=d+n(n-1)(g-1)
. The geometric
\sigma
-isotypic
X
-period defined in
?THM?
in this example is

(7.35)

\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{X,d}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}

where

(7.36)

\int_{X,d}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}:=\Gamma(\Bun_{n-1}^{d},\pi^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}|_{\Bun_{n-1}^{d}}),

and the main result in this section is the following (essentially proved in
[
24
]
):

Theorem 7.8
.

For each
d\in\mathbb{Z}
, there is a canonical isomorphism

\int_{X,d}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}\Sym^{d_{0}}(\Gamma(C,\sigma_{n-1}\otimes\sigma_{n})\langle 1\rangle)\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}})_{\Omega^{-n/2}}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle(n^{2}-2)(g-1)\rangle.

Proof of
?THM?
.

(7.37)

\displaystyle\int_{X,d}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}

(7.38)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1},\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\otimes r^{d,*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{FGV}})

(7.39)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1},\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\otimes s^{d,*}\rho_{n}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d,*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\mathrm{FGV}})

(7.40)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1},\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\otimes s^{d,*}v_{n,!}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle-d+(n-1)(g-1)\rangle)

(7.41)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1},\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\otimes p_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}s_{\mathrm{Q}}^{d,*}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle-d+(n-1)(g-1)\rangle)

(7.42)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1},p_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}(p_{n-1}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d,*}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\otimes s_{\mathrm{Q}}^{d,*}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}\allowbreak\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle-d+(n-1)(g-1)\rangle))

(7.43)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1},p_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}(v_{n-1}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d,*}v_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}})_{\Omega^{-n/2}}\otimes s_{\mathrm{Q}}^{d,*}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle-2d-(g-1)\rangle))

(7.44)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1}^{\prime\Omega^{-n/2},d},v_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}(v_{n-1}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d,*}v_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}})_{\Omega^{-n/2}}\otimes s_{\mathrm{Q}}^{d,*}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle-2d-(g-1)\rangle))

(7.45)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{c}(\Bun_{n-1}^{\prime\Omega^{-n/2},d},v_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n-1}}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}})_{\Omega^{-n/2}}\otimes v_{n-1,!}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}s_{\mathrm{Q}}^{d,*}\mathcal{W}_{\sigma_{n}}^{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2},d}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle-2d-(g-1)\rangle))

(7.46)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Gamma(C^{(d_{0})},(\sigma_{n-1}\otimes\sigma_{n})^{(d_{0})})\langle d_{0}\rangle\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}})_{\Omega^{-n/2}}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle(n^{2}-2)(g-1)\rangle

(7.47)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\to}}

\displaystyle\Sym^{d_{0}}(\Gamma(C,\sigma_{n-1}\otimes\sigma_{n})\langle 1\rangle)\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}})_{\Omega^{-n/2}}\otimes(\mathbb{L}_{\det\sigma_{n}})_{\Omega^{-(n-1)/2}}\langle(n^{2}-2)(g-1)\rangle

We make some remarks on this seemingly burdensome computation. The step (
7.41
) uses proper base change for the Cartesian square in (
7.34
). The step (
7.46
) is a direct consequence of
[
24
, §2.1 Main Local Theorem]
. All other steps are straightforward given
?THM?
and
?THM?
.

∎

Remark 7.9
.

For the convenience of the reader, we compare our notations with notations used in
[
24
]
. Our moduli space
\mathrm{Q}^{\Omega^{-n/2},d}_{n-1}
is isomorphic to
\mathchoice{\mathop{}\kern 12.12392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{n-1}}}{\mathop{}\kern 12.12392pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{n-1}}}{\mathop{}\kern 10.66797pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{n-1}}}{\mathop{}\kern 10.66797pt\mathopen{\vphantom{\mathcal{Q}}}_{\mathmakebox[0pt][r]{n-1}}}\mathcal{Q}_{d_{0}}
in
loc.cit
via tensoring

Remark 7.10
.

When
n=2
, the identity (

) is obvious, and we recommend the reader work through this case to see what is happening here.

7.3.
Obtaining higher period formulas

Now we combine all the input from
§7.1
and
§7.2
together, and prove the higher Rankin–Selberg period integral formula
?THM?
(
?THM?
).

Recall we use
g
to denote the genus of the curve
C
. Since there is no geometrically irreducible local system of rank
\geq 2
on
C
when
g=1
, we can assume
g\neq 1
.
Since
?THM?
is true in this case, we know that
?THM?
and
?THM?
are true. Then
?THM?
implies that
?THM?
is true for the local special cohomological correspondences
c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}
and
c_{W}^{\mathrm{l}}
constructed in (
7.1
).

Now, we can apply the construction in
§6.3.2
.
We take
K=V\oplus V^{*}
, where
V=\mathrm{Std}_{n}\boxtimes\mathrm{Std}_{n-1}
. Let
\lambda=\lambda_{\mathrm{Std}_{n-1}}\in X_{*}(T_{\GL_{n-1}})
. Then we have two non-degenerate bilinear forms
b=b_{\lambda,-\lambda}:V\otimes V^{*}\to\mathrm{triv}
and
b_{\mathrm{geo}}:V\otimes V^{*}\to\mathrm{triv}
defined in (
5.21
) and (
5.19
) respectively. These two bilinear forms give two symplectic forms
\omega_{M}:M^{\otimes 2}\to k
and
\omega_{M,\mathrm{geo}}:M^{\otimes 2}\to k
as in
§6.3.2
. Then
?THM?
implies
\omega_{M}=(-1)^{n-1}\omega_{M,\mathrm{geo}}
.

Using the local special cohomological correspondences
c_{V}^{\mathrm{l}}
and
c_{V^{*}}^{\mathrm{l}}
,
?THM?
makes
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
an
F
-equivariant
\operatorname{Cl}(M)
-module introduced in
?THM?
. To completely determine this module, we only need to compute the invariant
\lambda(\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}})
defined in (
3.24
).

Note that
\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}=\bigoplus_{d\in\mathbb{Z}}\int_{X,d}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
is naturally
\mathbb{Z}
-graded on which
\Gamma(C,V_{\sigma})\langle 1\rangle
acts by degree 1 endomorphisms and
\Gamma(C,V^{*}_{\sigma})\langle 1\rangle
acts by degree -1 endomorphisms. In particular, any non-zero vector in the one-dimensional subspace
\int_{X,2n(n-1)(g-1)}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}\subset\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}
will be a highest vector in the sense of
?THM?
. Then
?THM?
implies that

(7.48)

\begin{split}\lambda(\int_{X}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}})&=(F|_{\int_{X,2n(n-1)(g-1)}\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}}})^{2}\epsilon(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1})^{-1}\\
&=q^{-n^{2}(g-1)}\chi^{-n}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}}(\Omega)\chi^{-n+1}_{\det\sigma_{n}}(\Omega)\epsilon(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1})\end{split}

The Hecke characters
\chi_{\det\sigma_{n-1}}
,
\chi_{\det\sigma_{n}}
are introduced in
§2.10.1
.

Form the Kolyvagin system

(7.49)

\{z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r}\in M^{*\otimes r}\}_{r\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}}

as in (
6.8
). We can now apply
?THM?
and get the following:

Theorem 7.11
.

Under the setting above, the following identity holds for any
r\geq 0
:

(7.50)

\omega_{r,\mathrm{geo}}(z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r},z_{\mathbb{L}_{\sigma}^{\mathrm{FGV}},r})=\beta_{\sigma}(\log q)^{-r}\left(\frac{d}{ds}\right)^{r}\Big|_{s=0}(q^{2n(n-1)(g-1)s}L(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1},s+1/2)L(\sigma_{n}^{*}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}^{*},s+1/2))

where the bilinear forms
\omega_{r,\mathrm{geo}}
are induced from
\omega_{\mathrm{geo}}
, and

\beta_{\sigma}:=(-1)^{r/2}q^{-n^{2}(g-1)}\chi^{-n}_{\det\sigma_{n-1}}(\Omega)\chi^{-n+1}_{\det\sigma_{n}}(\Omega)\epsilon(\sigma_{n}\otimes\sigma_{n-1}).

Here the Hecke characters
\chi_{\det\sigma_{n-1}}
,
\chi_{\det\sigma_{n}}
are introduced in
§2.10.1
.

Remark 7.12
.

?THM?
gives us a simple description of the special cohomological correspondences

on the period sheaf

. This allows us to formulate
